## Where public sources contradict what the model believed

- Model believed (general): Tune IVF parameters relative to dataset size: pick nlist (number of inverted lists) proportional to sqrt(N) and set search params (nprobe) to trade recall vs latency; document chosen defaults and allow configuration. / Source says: When using IVF, choose numLists relative to dataset size (use documentCount/1000 for up to ~1M documents, and consider other index types like DiskANN beyond that); tune nProbes (nProbes/nProbes) to trade recall for latency rather than using a single universal sqrt(N) rule. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): When storing embeddings in BSON, use a deterministic binary representation (e.g., serialize float32 little-endian into BSON Binary subtype) and document the serialization format so clients and index creation use the same layout. / Source says: Store vectors as numeric arrays (number[]) so they can be indexed; embedding values stored as other types (for example raw BSON Binary) are not accepted by the vector index and will not be indexed. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Provision throughput appropriately for sustained ingestion: either provision collection/database throughput or use autoscale if available; monitor RU consumption (or equivalent) and scale before throttling becomes frequent. / Source says: Do not assume Azure DocumentDB uses the Cosmos DB 'RU' provisioning model; Azure DocumentDB uses selectable compute tiers and independently sized storage (vCores/tiers), so plan capacity by picking an appropriate cluster tier, storage and sharding approach rather than only thinking in RUs. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): If the managed service exposes a platform-native change feed (e.g., Cosmos DB Change Feed), evaluate using that instead of MongoDB change streams if it offers better SLAs/availability or integrates better with Azure tooling. / Source says: Azure DocumentDB exposes MongoDB change streams as the recommended change feed mechanism for MongoDB-compatible workloads; there is not a separate Cosmos DB change-feed alternative to prefer for this API. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Use ordered=False and writeConcern w/ appropriate w and wtimeout only if you understand tradeoffs; for maximum throughput prefer lower synchronous write guarantees but ensure app-level validation/compensation for data loss. / Source says: Azure's MongoDB-compatible service ignores client-specified MongoDB writeConcern (w/wtimeout); rely on account consistency settings and the platform's replication semantics rather than setting client writeConcern to tune durability. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Use the driver and cloud provider retry features: enable retryWrites/retryReads on the MongoDB driver, implement application-level retries with exponential backoff and jitter for transient errors, and only retry idempotent operations automatically. / Source says: K12: Use the driver and cloud provider retry features: enable retryWrites/retryReads on the MongoDB driver, implement application-level retries with exponential backoff and jitter for transient errors, and only retry idempotent operations automatically. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): For production throughput, prefer autoscale or provisioned RU/s appropriate to expected ingestion and LLM-driven write bursts; monitor RU consumption and add throttling/backpressure in the worker to avoid throttling errors. / Source says: K20: For production throughput, prefer autoscale or provisioned RU/s appropriate to expected ingestion and LLM-driven write bursts; monitor RU consumption and add throttling/backpressure in the worker to avoid throttling errors. (1 samples)
- Model believed (general): Use float32 arrays for embeddings (not strings) to reduce storage and RAM footprint, and persist embeddings in a consistent endianness and dtype expected by the vector index implementation. / Source says: Azure DocumentDB supports multiple vector numeric formats (including half-precision and product-quantized options) and recommends using lower-precision or quantized formats to reduce storage and indexing costs rather than mandating float32 only. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): For performance and cost reasons, batch inserts of document+embedding (e.g., 100–1000 docs per bulk insert) rather than inserting one document at a time during ingestion, and monitor RU/throughput or equivalent credits during bulk loads. / Source says: Azure DocumentDB storage uses premium SSD options and IOPS/bandwidth is gated by compute tier (not RU model); size compute for throughput requirements rather than sizing disk for IOPS. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Enable and rely on driver retryable reads/writes where available and implement additional idempotent-safe retries with exponential backoff for transient network/timeout errors (with jitter). / Source says: For many DocumentDB quickstarts and for vector-index operations, set retryWrites=false in the connection string (driver-managed write retries are often disabled for DocumentDB vector-search operations). (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Monitor RU/s and query latency in the Azure portal (or cluster tools) because multi-document transactions and large reads can dramatically increase RU consumption — provision or autoscale throughput accordingly and test under load. / Source says: Do not rely on monitoring 'RU/s' for Azure DocumentDB because DocumentDB's pricing and provisioning model is compute-and-storage based (vCore tiers and provisioned storage) rather than Request Unit (RU) throughput. (1 samples)
- Model believed (general): Read the cluster connection string from an environment variable (DOCUMENTDB_URI) and never hard-code it; create a single AsyncIOMotorClient per process and reuse it for all Beanie/ODM operations (close it on shutdown). / Source says: For Python apps prefer one process-global AsyncMongoClient (PyMongo's AsyncMongoClient) reused across the app lifecycle rather than creating a new Motor AsyncIOMotorClient per request; initialize once and close on shutdown. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Keep document sizes small (well below 2 MB); store large assets externally (blob storage) and keep references in the document to reduce RU cost and avoid item-size limits. / Source says: Azure DocumentDB enforces the MongoDB BSON document size limit (16 MB); keep documents well under 16 MB and spill large fields to a side collection or external blob storage. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Create and reuse a single Motor/MongoClient instance (singleton) for the whole FastAPI process and set an explicit connection pool size (e.g. maxPoolSize) rather than opening a new client per request. / Source says: PRIOR_K01: The public Azure DocumentDB guidance recommends using PyMongo's AsyncMongoClient (one process-wide client) rather than Motor; creating a Motor-specific singleton is not the recommended Python async pattern. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Set explicit readConcern/readPreference and writeConcern appropriate to your durability/latency needs (e.g. w=majority for stronger durability or w=1 for lower latency) and test behavior under expected failure modes. / Source says: PRIOR_K04: Azure DocumentDB maps/controls consistency itself and ignores client-specified writeConcern (writes are quorum/mapped to account consistency); do not rely on setting writeConcern on the client to achieve provider-level durability semantics. (1 samples)
- Model believed (general): Implement idempotent retries with exponential backoff for transient errors and use driver's retryWrites (and retryable reads) where supported; handle DuplicateKey and other non-idempotent errors explicitly instead of blind retries. / Source says: PRIOR_K05: Implement idempotent retries with exponential backoff for transient errors; do not rely on driver retryable writes on Azure DocumentDB (set retryWrites=false for many deployments) and use retryable reads where supported. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Provision and monitor throughput (RUs) for the database/collections and design queries to minimize RU cost: use projections to fetch only needed fields and avoid returning large documents unnecessarily. / Source says: Provision capacity by choosing the appropriate cluster compute tier (M10, M30, etc.) and storage sizing and monitor CPU/IOPS/storage/connection metrics (throughput is driven by compute/storage tier rather than Cosmos-style RU tokens); optimize queries to reduce IO and index usage. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Avoid long-running multi-document transactions across different partitions; prefer idempotent single-document updates or orchestrated workflows with compensation logic if multi-collection atomicity is required. / Source says: Azure DocumentDB supports multi-document transactions and the API exposes startSession()/withTransaction() that can run across multiple logical and physical shards (be aware of transaction timeouts and limits). (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Provision throughput (RU/s) appropriate for indexing and vector search: estimate ingestion, embedding compute and query load and use autoscale or set sufficient RU/s for the sample database/collection to avoid frequent 429s; increase RU during index creation and bulk loads. / Source says: Use cluster tier / vCore and storage sizing (M-tiers) to provision capacity for indexing and vector workloads; Azure DocumentDB uses cluster tiers (e.g., M30+) and not cosmos RU/s as the primary provisioning knob for the managed MongoDB-compatible service. (1 samples)
- Model believed (general): Limit document size: do not embed large binary or PDF blobs in documents; instead store large files in blob storage (Azure Blob) and include a reference (URL or id) in the DocumentDB document. / Source says: Do not store very large binary/PDF blobs inline in a hot collection; for Azure DocumentDB public guidance, prefer splitting very large fields into a side collection keyed by _id rather than co-locating large blobs in frequently-scanned collections. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Monitor request units / consumed throughput and configure autoscale or increase provisioned throughput for the sample_retail_08 database/collections to accommodate vector search and LLM-generation bursts; add alerts on RU consumption. / Source says: Azure DocumentDB uses compute tiers and per-shard IOPS/throughput characteristics rather than Cosmos DB-style RU accounting; monitor CPU/memory/IOPS and scale the compute/storage tier and alerts accordingly. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Provision collection throughput (RU/s) or autoscale appropriately for the de-identification pipeline's read/write pattern; benchmark and raise RUs or enable autoscale to avoid frequent throttling during batch runs. / Source says: Size cluster capacity by picking the appropriate compute tier and storage type (IOPS/bandwidth are gated by compute tier for Premium SSD v2) and use autoscale or larger tiers for heavy batch workloads rather than thinking in Cosmos DB RU/s. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Monitor query RU charge and latency (via driver command monitoring or Azure Monitor/Diagnostic settings); add alerts for sustained high RU consumption or frequent 429s and capture slow query patterns to optimize indexes/queries. / Source says: Enable diagnostic settings and slow-query logging and alert on CPU, memory, IOPS, storage, and connection saturation (and frequent 429s); use Log Analytics for slow-query analysis rather than relying on RU metrics. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Tune provisioning/throughput for the collection: for Azure Cosmos-style clusters provision RU/s or autoscale appropriate for expected aggregate queries and writes; avoid surprise 429s by estimating RU cost of the aggregation and increasing throughput or splitting queries. / Source says: Tune cluster compute tier and storage (vCore/tiers) and monitor CPU, memory and IOPS to scale appropriately (Azure DocumentDB uses compute tiers and storage; it does not rely on RU/s provisioning like Cosmos RU models). (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Set secure connection options: require TLS/SSL, enable retryable writes if supported, set reasonable timeouts (serverSelectionTimeoutMS, connectTimeoutMS), and configure maxPoolSize/minPoolSize to match expected concurrency. / Source says: Require TLS in the connection string and use the service-recommended driver options such as retryWrites=false and maxIdleTimeMS (do not enable driver-level retryWrites by default for DocumentDB without checking compatibility). (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Set appropriate writeConcern and readConcern for transactional work (use writeConcern: { w: 'majority' } and readConcern: 'local' or 'majority' per consistency requirements) and ensure driver options include retryWrites=true for transient server errors. / Source says: Do not assume driver-managed retryable writes are enabled; Azure DocumentDB samples set retryWrites=false and recommend designing idempotent application-level retries rather than relying on retryWrites=true for non-idempotent operations. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Enable server-side retryWrites and configure the driver to use sensible serverSelectionTimeoutMS and socketTimeoutMS to avoid long request hangs; handle DuplicateKey and idempotency explicitly for write retries. / Source says: Set retryWrites=false in the connection string for Azure DocumentDB and do not rely on the driver's automatic retryWrites behavior for non-idempotent operations. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Use writeConcern 'majority' (or the equivalent durable write setting) for portfolio upserts when the application requires read-after-write consistency, or explicitly read with majority readConcern after a critical write. / Source says: Don't assume client-specified MongoDB writeConcern will control durability; Azure DocumentDB maps/makes writes quorum/majority at the service level and client writeConcern may be ignored—use account-level consistency settings to reason about read-after-write behavior. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Use driver‑level options for retryable reads/writes where supported (retryReads/retryWrites) and be aware of differences in retry semantics between MongoDB drivers and Azure's MongoDB API; test retry behavior for your driver version. / Source says: Set retryWrites=false in the connection string for Azure DocumentDB because the service does not support MongoDB retryable writes; do not rely on driver-level retryable-writes semantics without verifying service support. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Set appropriate write concern and read concern for your risk-review workload (e.g., majority or w=1 depending on durability vs latency needs) and document this in configuration. / Source says: Azure DocumentDB maps MongoDB writeConcern/readConcern to the service consistency model and the account's consistency settings; client-specified writeConcern may be ignored, so use and document the account-level consistency configuration rather than relying on driver writeConcern to enforce durability. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Enable retryable writes and implement application-level retries with exponential backoff for transient network or rate-limited errors; make write operations idempotent when possible (e.g., upsert with a unique client-generated id). / Source says: Set retryWrites=false in the MongoDB connection string for Azure DocumentDB and implement application-level retries with exponential backoff and idempotent writes instead of relying on driver-managed retryWrites. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Store vector embeddings as a fixed-length float32 array with an explicit binary BSON subtype (use the provider-supported subtype, e.g., 0x09 if the server expects it), and record the embedding dimension in the document schema to validate clients (e.g., {embeddingDim:512}). / Source says: Store vectors as a JSON numeric array (number[]) in the document field so they can be indexed by cosmosSearch; non-number types (or unsupported encodings) may prevent a document from being indexed. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): When packing embeddings into BSON binary ensure byte order and type match server expectations (float32 little-endian); use a consistent serializer (e.g., numpy.astype('float32').tobytes()) and validate roundtrip reads in tests. / Source says: Prefer storing vectors as arrays of numeric values (float32/float16) in fields and use the documented vector-field encodings/options (float types, half/quantization) rather than relying on an undocumented BSON binary packing; verify encoding support in the service docs before using a binary subtype. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Avoid multi-document transactions spanning partitions for CLV writes; prefer single-partition updates or design for per-customer partitioning—if multi-document transactions are needed, validate whether the MongoDB API on this cluster supports them before relying on them. / Source says: Azure DocumentDB supports multi-document ACID transactions across collections and shards (the platform documents multi-document transactions as supported). (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Monitor RU (request unit) consumption and provisioned throughput; run explain() on heavy queries and add/adjust indexes or increase throughput if you hit 429s for read/write patterns from the demo or API endpoints. / Source says: Do not assume Azure DocumentDB exposes Cosmos DB RUs; monitor node/cluster metrics (CPU, memory, IOPS) and slow-query logs rather than tracking RU consumption which is a Cosmos DB concept. (1 samples)
- Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Instrument and log RU consumption per operation and include request metrics (latency, status) in monitoring so you can correlate API usage patterns with RU spikes and tune throughput accordingly. / Source says: Instrument and log resource/operation metrics (latency, success/failure, background-job lag); DocumentDB docs recommend diagnostic settings and slow-query logs rather than RU-per-op metrics. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): For reconciliations that update multiple documents (invoice flagged matched and PO annotated), use multi-document transactions (startSession + withTransaction) so the set of writes is consistent if the cluster supports MongoDB transactions. / Source says: Azure DocumentDB supports multi-document transactions but with limits: multi-document (ACID) transactions are supported only within a single non-sharded collection (cross-collection and cross-shard transactions are not supported). (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Enable retryable writes (retryWrites=true) and idempotent write patterns in the client when supported by the server to reduce error handling complexity for transient failures. / Source says: Do not assume MongoDB driver's retryable-writes should be enabled by default for Azure DocumentDB; many Azure samples set retryWrites=false and some features (vector index workflows) require retryWrites=false. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Use multi-document transactions when creating the household document and updating customer documents in a single logical operation to maintain consistency (commit both the household doc and member updates in one transaction). / Source says: Multi-document transactions are limited: use transactions only within a single (nonsharded) collection; cross-collection multi-document transactions are not supported. (1 samples)
- Model believed (mongodb): Use an appropriate write concern (acknowledged writes or w: 'majority' where durability matters) for household creation and membership changes; evaluate performance impact before using majority everywhere. / Source says: Client-specified writeConcern is ignored by the service: Azure DocumentDB maps writes to its own consistency model (quorum/Session); do not rely on client writeConcern settings like w:'majority' to change durability semantics on the service. (1 samples)

- Model believed (mongodb): For multi-document transactions, always start the transaction on a session from the same client/connection and ensure the involved collections are in the same database and appropriate partition/shard so the transaction is supported; keep transactions short and touch as few documents as possible. / Source says: Azure DocumentDB supports multi-document ACID transactions (use startSession()/withTransaction and pass the session to each operation) and transactions may span collections and shards; use the driver's transaction APIs to commit or retry. - Model believed (mongodb): When running heavy aggregation pipelines, push $match and $project stages as early as possible to reduce working set, use allowDiskUse when needed for large groups/sorts, and monitor RU consumption for the pipeline. / Source says: When running aggregation pipelines, push $match and $project stages as early as possible, use allowDiskUse for large groups or sorts, and verify the plan with explain() to avoid COLLSCANs and blocking in-memory sorts. - Model believed (azure_cosmos_db): Monitor RU/s, request charge per query, index usage, latency, and throttling metrics in Azure Monitor and instrument the app to log RU costs returned by the server for expensive aggregation runs. / Source says: Do not assume Cosmos DB-specific metrics (RUs/request-charge) apply; Azure DocumentDB uses compute + storage billing and exposes direct cluster metrics (CPU, memory, IOPS) instead. - Model believed (mongodb): For dedupe/merge operations, prefer single‑document atomic updates or use findAndModify / find_one_and_update patterns that return the post‑update document rather than multi‑document cross‑partition transactions; design data to minimize cross‑partition transactions. / Source says: Azure DocumentDB supports multi-document ACID transactions (use startSession()/withTransaction in drivers) including cross-collection and cross-shard transactions where supported; use driver sessions and transaction APIs to group operations when atomicity across documents is required. ### Known to the model, no public source, not on the checklist

- Use an explicit, consistent document schema for fraud/transaction data and include schemaVersion for migrations. (1 samples)
- Do not log protected health information (PHI) or patient-identifying fields to application logs or error traces; mask/redact such fields in logs and treat clinical text as sensitive. (1 samples)
- Keep vector index metadata (algorithm, dimensions, metric) and the client-side embedding preprocessing (normalization) in versioned config/source control so ingestion and index assumptions remain aligned. (1 samples)
- Enforce patient-level authorization in the application layer in addition to database filters: verify the authenticated user is authorized to read a given patientId before issuing database queries. (1 samples)
- If you rely on Cosmos DB RU-based guidance in memory, note that Azure DocumentDB uses cluster tiers and compute/storage sizing rather than the Cosmos RU billing model. (1 samples)
- The Azure DocumentDB public docs do not prescribe a specific client-side ANN library (FAISS/hnswlib) as the official fallback; the service docs focus on server-side vector indexes instead. (1 samples)
- Model eligibility criteria in a structured, queryable form (normalized fields and arrays) instead of opaque free text so deterministic rule engines can apply inclusion/exclusion logic. (1 samples)
- Avoid multi-document transactions for high-throughput ingestion; prefer single-document writes and idempotent design. (1 samples)
- Include a fixed taxonomy in the prompt and validate that the model's output strictly matches one of the taxonomy values before writing to the DB. (1 samples)
- Protect PII before sending text to an external LLM: redact or hash personally-identifying fields (account numbers, full names) in the prompt or obtain explicit consent and document the data sent to the model. (1 samples)
- Use explicit client sessions and withTransaction to group multi-document operations, and pass the session to every operation in the transaction callback. (1 samples)
- Keep detailed immutable audit metadata on each Transaction (createdAt, createdBy, transactionRef...). (1 samples)
- Avoid TTL indexes on immutable ledgers. (1 samples)
- Make webhook delivery idempotent (include an event id or deduplication key and have the receiver deduplicate), or persist short-lived delivery records so retries don't cause duplicate effects. (1 samples)
- When using Mongoose in read-heavy code paths, prefer projection + .lean() to avoid constructing Mongoose documents when you only need plain objects for evaluation/aggregation. (1 samples)
- Write unit tests for aggregation logic with realistic sample documents and include short integration tests that run aggregations against the live sample_fintech_03 database to validate correctness and index usage. (1 samples)
- Keep alert evaluation fast by precomputing thresholds and caching static lookups (instrument metadata like sector) with an in-memory TTL cache. (1 samples)
- Azure DocumentDB storage uses premium SSD options and IOPS/bandwidth is gated by compute tier (not RU model). (1 samples)
- Sanitize and normalize text before embedding (strip control characters, normalize whitespace, preserve timestamps). (1 samples)
- Assemble retrieved passages to fit LLM token budget: rank by vector score, concatenate until budget minus answer buffer is reached, prefer distinct timestamps. (1 samples)
- Use deterministic pseudo-embeddings when API keys are unavailable, but document this and ensure production uses real embeddings. (1 samples)
- Record each orchestration step's status and timestamps in a workflow-run document to support resumability and idempotent retries. (1 samples)
- Use multi-document transactions only when required for atomicity across documents/collections, keep transactions small and short-lived, and validate transaction behavior against the live cluster. (1 samples)
- Do not write secrets or raw PII into workflow logs or plain documents; redact or encrypt sensitive fields and restrict access via RBAC and network controls. (1 samples)
- Run integration tests against a real DocumentDB instance (or the documented local Gateway) for behaviors mongomock cannot emulate (indexing, explain plans, vector indexes, transactions). (1 samples)
- Persist only minimal identifiers for agent conversational memory (conversationId, lastAppointmentId) and keep full conversational context ephemeral; encrypt persisted sensitive state at rest when required. (1 samples)
- When rescheduling slot times, check for overlapping slots for the same provider using indexed queries and enforce application-level checks to prevent overlapping bookings because DB-level partial unique constraints on time ranges are not a general replacement. (1 samples)
- Rate-limit and validate user inputs on expensive endpoints (e.g., refresh and ask); authenticate and authorize refresh operations to avoid accidental large refresh jobs or expensive LLM/vector queries from untrusted callers. (1 samples)
- When public guidance is absent for framework-specific initialization (e.g., Beanie-specific init rules), follow framework docs and treat service-level guidance as application policy. (1 samples)
- Normalize natural-language time references (like 'last month') to explicit ISO date ranges and store in conversation state. (1 samples)
- Limit concurrency when calling external services (OpenAI) using an async semaphore or similar rate-limiter to bound simultaneous requests so you don't overwhelm external APIs or your pipeline. (1 samples)
- Prefer extracting structured tags/key themes and indexing those tag fields for fast filtering/aggregation rather than relying solely on expensive free-text indexes. (1 samples)
- Test pipelines end-to-end with a mockable OpenAI implementation in CI (DevProxy / local LLM / test doubles) so CI runs do not require real API keys and real external calls. (1 samples)
- Perform input validation and sanitize free-text prior to passing it to an LLM and avoid storing unnecessary PII; for data-at-rest encryption of sensitive fields prefer customer-managed keys (CMK) where compliance requires it. (1 samples)
- Store monetary amounts using Decimal128 or integer minor-units; do not use JavaScript Number for currency values. (1 samples)
- When using Mongoose with sessions, pass { session } to all queries/updates and use session.withTransaction or explicit startTransaction/commitTransaction. (1 samples)
- If createSearchIndexes isn't available, implement a graceful fallback (store embeddings and client-side NN). (1 samples)
- When enriching with LLMs store source_text, extracted fields, and model metadata so reprocessing is possible. (1 samples)
- Do not log PHI or sensitive patient data and redact or exclude identifying fields from diagnostics and logs; ensure log retention and access meet compliance requirements. (1 samples)
- Persist intermediate orchestrator state (reviewResult, letterDraft) so orchestrator logic is resumable and idempotent, allowing retries without duplication. (1 samples)
- When updating shuffled/competing documents concurrently use optimistic concurrency (version) or a conditional findOneAndUpdate with version-check to avoid lost updates; the service itself does not mandate a single pattern so implement per-app. (1 samples)
- Azure DocumentDB uses compute tiers and per-shard IOPS/throughput characteristics rather than Cosmos DB-style RU accounting; monitor CPU/memory/IOPS and scale the compute/storage tier and alerts accordingly. (1 samples)
- Design writeConcern and readPreference intentionally for cross-replica durability or latency needs, but verify Azure DocumentDB's default mapping and supported features first. (1 samples)
- Avoid sending raw PII to LLMs or retaining it in logs; redact or minimize PII in prompts and encrypt or limit sensitive fields at rest. (1 samples)
- Before sending clinical text to an external LLM, verify compliance and minimize PHI exposure. (1 samples)
- Create the Mongo/PyMongo client with explicit connection-tuning options: set a reasonable maxPoolSize, minPoolSize, serverSelectionTimeoutMS and socketTimeoutMS to avoid long blocking on network failures. (1 samples)
- The two-step z-score outlier pattern (compute mean/stdDev in a sub-pipeline then join to per-day buckets) is a valid MongoDB design pattern, but there is no Azure DocumentDB-specific public document that prescribes this exact implementation; treat it as a generic MongoDB technique rather than an Azure DocumentDB-specific guidance. (1 samples)
- Use server-side summarization or optional LLM call only after local rule-based summarization fallback: ensure the pipeline returns a compact summary payload to the summarizer, and guard LLM calls with token/length limits and API key checks. (1 samples)
- Implement optimistic concurrency control when updating lead documents (e.g., include a version field or make updates conditional). (1 samples)
- Protect PII before sending data to an LLM (redact or mask identifiers if not needed). (1 samples)
- Guard LLM calls behind feature flags, quotas and validation and provide deterministic fallbacks when the LLM or API key is unavailable. (1 samples)
- When writing a score back to a lead document, use an atomic update (e.g., findOneAndUpdate / $set) and include audit metadata such as scoredAt, scorerId and modelVersion so scoring is traceable. (1 samples)
- Rate-limit and cache calls to external embedding providers, and handle 429s with backoff. (1 samples)
- Redact or avoid logging full clinical intent texts and embedding vectors in persistent logs; treat clinical queries as sensitive. (1 samples)
- Persist agent/session memory or ephemeral classification decisions with versioning and, if ephemeral, a TTL; include deterministic-fallback flags. (1 samples)
- Require a tenantId equality predicate on every read/write operation and enforce it centrally (e.g., a Mongoose repository-layer plugin that injects { tenantId } into queries and rejects any operation that would run without it). (1 samples)
- Detect and block dangerous repository operations (e.g., updateMany, deleteMany without tenantId) via code review or automated tests; include unit/integration tests that assert tenant isolation (queries cannot return results from other tenantIds). (1 samples)
- Use deterministic pseudo-embeddings only for local unit tests and CI; in integration or production use a real embedding model and store a model identifier and version with each vector. (1 samples)
- Keep embeddings in a dedicated collection (prior belief). (1 samples)
- Use a dedicated database and well‑named collections for an application rather than writing into shared or admin databases (e.g., use database sample_healthcare_11 and create separate collections for patients and visits). (1 samples)
- For PHI and healthcare data do not send identifiable patient data to third‑party LLMs unless you have an appropriate HIPAA-compliant contract and proper de‑identification; prefer private/managed LLMs or redact PHI before external calls. (1 samples)
- When official server-side vector search is unavailable in a deployment, implement a documented fallback and mark results as coming from the fallback. (1 samples)
- If application requires deterministic, auditable review scoring, persist matched_ids, distances/searchScore, matched text snippets and run metadata alongside the review results. (1 samples)
- Do not return full embedding vectors in API responses by default; return only metadata and a score unless clients explicitly need the raw embedding. (1 samples)
- Record LLM outputs and the LLM rationale in structured fields and an append-only audit or history subdocument rather than overwriting a single source-of-truth field. (1 samples)
- Sanitize and validate any values produced by an LLM before writing them to the database (type checking, allowed enums, ranges). (1 samples)
- Use Mongoose schema validation (required fields, enums for stage names, types) and set strict mode to reject unexpected fields to prevent malformed or partial audit events being recorded. (1 samples)
- Design change-stream consumers to be idempotent (dedupe or use idempotency keys) because event replays or redelivery are possible and can otherwise cause double-counting. (1 samples)
- Normalize vendor names on write (e.g., lowercase/trim/canonicalize) if you need reliable equality or indexed lookups rather than relying on ad-hoc regex at query time. (1 samples)
- Record embedding metadata (dimension and model version) in your application documents to detect incompatible embeddings before re-indexing. (1 samples)
- Compute aggregates like win rate, average and total P&L using server-side aggregation pipelines ($match → $group → $project) and retrieve ordered series for cumulative/drawdown calculations; for very large aggregations consider allowDiskUse for server-side sorts or spilling. (1 samples)
- For server-side cumulative metrics and drawdown, prefer $setWindowFields if the cluster supports it; if not available compute cumulative series client-side after retrieving an ordered series from the DB. (1 samples)
- Enforce collection-level validation (JSON Schema) to require and type-check fields such as strategy_id, event_type, timestamp and pnl to prevent invalid events from being stored. (1 samples)
- Avoid sending raw PII or sensitive balance-level data to external LLM services; minimize exposure by sending de-identified aggregates or running LLMs in an approved, private environment. (1 samples)
- Use deterministic offline embedding and agent fallbacks for unit tests to avoid hitting external LLM/embedding services, and assert retrieval logic independently from upstream model availability. (1 samples)
- Do not assume MongoDB driver's retryable-writes should be enabled by default for Azure DocumentDB; many Azure samples set retryWrites=false and some features (vector index workflows) require retryWrites=false. (1 samples)
- Multi-document transactions are limited: use transactions only within a single (nonsharded) collection; cross-collection multi-document transactions are not supported. (1 samples)
- Keep the sample confined to a single dedicated database name (sample_customer_360_13) and avoid cross-database operations. (1 samples)
- Verify current driver/Mongoose flags (useNewUrlParser/useUnifiedTopology) compatibility before changing them; driver options have evolved across versions. (1 samples)
- Do not expose raw DB keys or connection strings to external models or agents; instead wire models to parameterized tools that perform DB access on the model's behalf. (1 samples)
- If doing client-side reranking, fetch a modest candidate set and pre-filter by indexed metadata. (1 samples)
- Design a loan-application document to contain id, current state, schema version, transitions, timestamps, etc. (1 samples)
- Implement optimistic concurrency control by including an expected state or version in update filters and retrying on mismatch. (1 samples)
- When using Beanie or another ODM, register document models and create indexes at startup (e.g., call init_beanie / create_indexes). (1 samples)
- Use optimistic concurrency (version or lastUpdated checked in update predicate) (1 samples)
- When using Beanie with an async driver, initialize Beanie once at startup with your Motor/Async client and document models and reuse the client (1 samples)
- In CI, mock the DB and driver sessions for unit tests and run integration tests that seed and mutate a live cluster only when permitted (1 samples)
- Store vectors in a binary/typed float representation (e.g., Float32 arrays) and keep a documented dimension field so index creation and queries use the correct dimensionality. (1 samples)
- Prefer explicit, documented guidance for readPreference/readConcern only when the service documents their mapping for the MongoDB driver; do not assume MongoDB defaults map identically without checking Azure DocumentDB compatibility docs. (1 samples)
- Use .lean() for read-heavy queries and project only required fields. (1 samples)
- Do not assume driver writeConcern settings (w, j) will be honored identically; verify the service's behavior before relying on w:'majority' or j:true. (1 samples)
- Protect the LangChain/OpenAI integration: require OPENAI_API_KEY in env, validate presence at startup, never log the key, and implement rate-limiting and per-user quota controls for marketer-facing natural-language queries. (1 samples)
- Deterministic cache key for materialized summary ids (1 samples)
- Use bulk writes to improve throughput and handle BulkWriteError and examine upserted_ids for idempotency verification. (1 samples)
- Choose a chunk size tuned to your embedding model and record chunk length/token count in metadata for debugging. (1 samples)
- When ingesting many documents use batched bulk writes (driver bulk APIs) and moderate batch sizes. (1 samples)
- Batch large seed ingestion carefully and monitor for throttling; prefer bulkWrite in batches and back off on rate-limiting errors. (1 samples)
- Application-level external search calls should use timeouts, retries, and fallbacks (mock) so the agent remains responsive. (1 samples)
- Store temporal fields in UTC using BSON Date objects and design seasonal comparisons with explicit date ranges. (1 samples)
- Treat a recommended audit document shape (immutable, run_id, created_at, schema_version, scenario_parameters, agent_outputs, aggregated_results, provenance) as an application-level convention. (1 samples)
- For unit tests prefer lightweight mocks or run integration tests against an isolated live test database. (1 samples)
- Keep a canonical merged profile per customer and store provenance/audit records (merge history) in a separate collection rather than deleting originals. (1 samples)
- Persist deterministic matching metadata (scores, model/prompt/version) for reproducibility and audit of LLM-assisted merges. (1 samples)
- Validate and canonicalize input identifiers at ingest (normalize emails/phones, store normalized keys and optional hashed keys). (1 samples)
- Treat LLM-driven extraction outputs as untrusted: validate, canonicalize, map to controlled vocabularies, log parse confidence, and quarantine ambiguous results rather than writing them directly to production structured fields. (1 samples)
- If storing PHI consider client-side field-level encryption and avoid printing PHI in logs. (1 samples)
- Include a schema_version attribute in documents and design migration/upgrade paths. (1 samples)
- Record provenance (model, prompt, embedding model/version, timestamp) alongside LLM-generated documents. (1 samples)
- Store raw embeddings as a binary-efficient float32 representation rather than JSON strings and ensure drivers/servers preserve numeric precision needed for vector search. (1 samples)
- If a third-party embedder is temporarily unavailable, allow ingestion to persist feature documents without embeddings and flag them for later enrichment/backfill rather than blocking ingestion. (1 samples)
- Make upserts idempotent by using a deterministic unique key (for example a composed _id or a unique compound key built from underlying+expiry+strike+optionType) so retries and duplicate ingestion don't create duplicate documents. (1 samples)
- Use pagination or cursor-based iteration (limit+sort, range queries, or server-side cursors) for endpoints that may return large result sets rather than returning arbitrarily large lists in a single HTTP response. (1 samples)
- Choose and be explicit about write durability/consistency settings appropriate to your workload, but verify how Azure DocumentDB maps or supports MongoDB writeConcern before relying on non-default settings. (1 samples)
- Avoid multi-document transactions for common read-heavy vector-search paths; prefer single-document atomic operations and optimistic concurrency where possible. (1 samples)
- When using Mongoose in production, avoid enabling autoIndex; prefer controlled index builds as part of deployment. (1 samples)
- Validate and sanitize latitude/longitude input ranges (lat between -90 and 90, lon between -180 and 180) and enforce a reasonable maximum radius for geospatial queries to prevent abusive or expensive queries. (1 samples)
- Check cluster tier and service version for DiskANN support before index creation, failing clearly without substituting another index. (1 samples)
- Use consistent embedding model/preprocessing and reject mismatched dimensions and nonfinite vector elements. (1 samples)
- Store one embedding-bearing document per product and purchase transactions separately, rather than treating every order as a searchable product. (1 samples)
- Overfetch candidates, exclude the input and duplicate IDs, apply co-purchase/session reranking, then select five. (1 samples)
- Validate URI configuration, await a bounded connect/ping, and distinguish missing, malformed, authentication and connectivity failures without exposing credentials. (1 samples)
- Set finite server-selection, connect and wait-queue timeouts and maxTimeMS on expensive queries. (1 samples)
- Use deterministic order IDs and updateOne/$setOnInsert upserts for repeatable transaction seeding. (1 samples)
- Match containing orders first and count each companion product at most once per order. (1 samples)
- Project only ranking/explanation fields and exclude embeddings and raw orders from responses and LLM context. (1 samples)
- Use authenticated ownership or unguessable session IDs and bound memory history and lifetime. (1 samples)
- Ground explanations in retrieved attributes/counts/session preferences and validate returned IDs against ranked candidates. (1 samples)
- Use idempotent setup comparing existing index definitions and verify vector readiness before recommendations. (1 samples)
- Persist model identity/version and source-content hash, regenerating embeddings when source content or model changes. (1 samples)

## Best practices that could not be assessed

- Tune client connection-pool and timeout options to match workload. (1 samples)
- Tune search-time ANN parameters and keep lSearch >= k; choose conservative topK and adjust ef/probe for recall vs latency. (1 samples)
- Provision cluster compute/tier to match expected search and ingestion load (choose an appropriate tier such as M30+ for production vector workloads). (1 samples)
- Do not log protected health information (PHI) or patient-identifying fields to application logs or error traces; mask/redact such fields in logs and treat clinical text as sensitive. (1 samples)
- Keep vector index metadata (algorithm, dimensions, metric) and the client-side embedding preprocessing (normalization) in versioned config/source control so ingestion and index assumptions remain aligned. (1 samples)
- Enforce patient-level authorization in the application layer in addition to database filters: verify the authenticated user is authorized to read a given patientId before issuing database queries. (1 samples)
- If you rely on Cosmos DB RU-based guidance in memory, note that Azure DocumentDB uses cluster tiers and compute/storage sizing rather than the Cosmos RU billing model. (1 samples)
- For paging through large result sets, prefer range- or cursor-based (seek) pagination rather than skip/offset-heavy pagination to keep queries efficient. (1 samples)
- The Azure DocumentDB public docs do not prescribe a specific client-side ANN library (FAISS/hnswlib) as the official fallback; the service docs focus on server-side vector indexes instead. (1 samples)
- Model eligibility criteria in a structured, queryable form (normalized fields and arrays) instead of opaque free text so deterministic rule engines can apply inclusion/exclusion logic. (1 samples)
- Samples often set retryWrites=false in connection strings and note that driver-managed retryable writes differ from application-level retries; verify retry semantics for your deployment and ensure any application retries are safe and idempotent. (1 samples)
- You can create only one vector index per vector path on a collection; plan index lifecycle accordingly. (1 samples)
- Avoid multi-document transactions for high-throughput ingestion; prefer single-document writes and idempotent design. (1 samples)
- Protect PII before sending text to an external LLM: redact or hash personally-identifying fields (account numbers, full names) in the prompt or obtain explicit consent and document the data sent to the model. (1 samples)
- Set retryWrites=false in the Azure DocumentDB connection string and verify driver-level support and implications for write retries before enabling driver-managed retryable writes. (1 samples)
- Use explain('executionStats') / query plans to validate that queries are using indexes and to diagnose COLLSCAN or in-memory sorts. (1 samples)
- Make webhook delivery idempotent (include an event id or deduplication key and have the receiver deduplicate), or persist short-lived delivery records so retries don't cause duplicate effects. (1 samples)
- When using Mongoose in read-heavy code paths, prefer projection + .lean() to avoid constructing Mongoose documents when you only need plain objects for evaluation/aggregation. (1 samples)
- Keep alert evaluation fast by precomputing thresholds and caching static lookups (instrument metadata like sector) with an in-memory TTL cache. (1 samples)
- Monitor index and query performance and use the MCP inspection tools to validate created indexes and query plans. (1 samples)
- Use the DocumentDB MCP server tools to inspect index definitions, validate document counts, and verify ingestion in automation. (1 samples)
- Enforce TLS on client connections by using tls=true or ssl=true in the MongoDB URI so communication with the Azure DocumentDB cluster is encrypted. (1 samples)
- Run explain(..., 'executionStats') on representative queries to confirm index usage before shipping. (1 samples)
- Use least-privilege credentials: prefer Microsoft Entra ID (managed identity) + RBAC for apps, or create dedicated DB users with only required roles and rotate secrets via Key Vault. (1 samples)
- Enable diagnostic settings and slow-query logging (route to Log Analytics) and use explain()/operation logs to identify slow queries and drive index changes and alerts on saturation metrics. (1 samples)
- Enable in-region high availability (HA) on production clusters to obtain the 99.99% SLA and automatic zone-level failover; add cross-region replicas for DR and higher SLAs when needed. (1 samples)
- For regulated workloads, enable customer-managed keys (CMK) at cluster creation to control encryption-at-rest keys and plan key rotation and recovery runbooks. (1 samples)
- Use multi-document transactions only when required for atomicity across documents/collections, keep transactions small and short-lived, and validate transaction behavior against the live cluster. (1 samples)
- Persist only minimal identifiers for agent conversational memory (conversationId, lastAppointmentId) and keep full conversational context ephemeral; encrypt persisted sensitive state at rest when required. (1 samples)
- When rescheduling slot times, check for overlapping slots for the same provider using indexed queries and enforce application-level checks to prevent overlapping bookings because DB-level partial unique constraints on time ranges are not a general replacement. (1 samples)
- When using vector search, store embeddings as numeric arrays and create a DocumentDB vector index with the exact dimensions and similarity (COS/L2/IP); prefer DiskANN for production scale and tune maxDegree, lBuild and query-time lSearch for the recall/latency tradeoff. (1 samples)
- Rate-limit and validate user inputs on expensive endpoints (e.g., refresh and ask); authenticate and authorize refresh operations to avoid accidental large refresh jobs or expensive LLM/vector queries from untrusted callers. (1 samples)
- Keep an index budget per collection (review $indexStats) and prefer 5–15 well-chosen indexes; hide/drop unused indexes rather than accumulating overlapping single-field indexes. (1 samples)
- Always connect with TLS and use the mongodb+srv scheme and tls=true in the connection string when connecting to an Azure DocumentDB cluster. (1 samples)
- For many DocumentDB quickstarts and for vector-index operations, set retryWrites=false in the connection string (driver-managed write retries are often disabled for DocumentDB vector-search operations). (1 samples)
- Tune vector-index algorithm and parameters to your data and tier: prefer DiskANN for large/production (M30+), HNSW for high-recall on M30+, IVF for small/test datasets; tune numLists (IVF), M and efConstruction (HNSW), and lBuild/lSearch/maxDegree (DiskANN). (1 samples)
- Validate heavy queries and aggregation pipelines with explain('executionStats') to confirm index usage and avoid COLLSCANs. (1 samples)
- Use the service-provided tooling (MCP server) to inspect live collections, index definitions and query plans as part of verification and troubleshooting. (1 samples)
- Run explain() / executionStats for representative queries during development to confirm intended indexes are used and to detect COLLSCANs. (1 samples)
- Use least-privilege credentials for application access: create nonadministrative users/roles with only the minimum DB privileges required instead of using admin/master credentials. (1 samples)
- Use JSON schema validation ($jsonSchema) on collections where supported to enforce required fields and types, or validate in the application layer before writes. (1 samples)
- Limit concurrency when calling external services (OpenAI) using an async semaphore or similar rate-limiter to bound simultaneous requests so you don't overwhelm external APIs or your pipeline. (1 samples)

- Store temporal fields in UTC using BSON Date objects and design seasonal comparisons with explicit date ranges. (1 samples)
- Use explain('executionStats') or the MCP explain_operation tool to verify index usage for aggregation pipelines when tuning. (1 samples)
- Create vector indexes through the provider APIs (createIndexes / cosmosSearch options) and tune algorithm-specific parameters. (1 samples)
- Keep a canonical merged profile per customer and store provenance/audit records (merge history) in a separate collection rather than deleting originals. (1 samples)
- Persist deterministic matching metadata (scores, model/prompt/version) for reproducibility and audit of LLM-assisted merges. (1 samples)
- Validate and canonicalize input identifiers at ingest (normalize emails/phones, store normalized keys and optional hashed keys). (1 samples)
- Use explain('executionStats') and examine keysExamined/docsExamined and winningPlan to verify index usage and identify COLLSCAN or scatter-gather behavior before adding indexes. (1 samples)
- Restrict network access: during development add your client IP to firewall rules, and for production prefer Private Endpoint (Private Link) instead of opening public access to the cluster. (1 samples)
- Run explain('executionStats') and use $indexStats to inspect execution plans, measure totalDocsExamined/totalKeysExamined, and confirm index selection before changing indexes. (1 samples)
- Always page large result sets (use limit + sort + range filters or cursor.batchSize) and avoid unbounded server-side scans that stream huge result sets into application memory. (1 samples)
- Validate and whitelist queryable fields and use parameterized queries or typed filters to avoid injection risks instead of allowing raw query JSON from untrusted inputs. (1 samples)
- Use the mongodb+srv scheme in connection URIs so drivers can resolve the best writable host in multi-cluster configurations and to support global read-write endpoints. (1 samples)
- Use Index Advisor and $indexStats to identify unused indexes and to tune your index set rather than relying solely on rules of thumb. (1 samples)
- When using LangChain, prefer pre_filter (or driver-level filter) to narrow candidates inside vector search (1 samples)
- If storing PHI consider client-side field-level encryption and avoid printing PHI in logs. (1 samples)
- Include a schema_version attribute in documents and design migration/upgrade paths. (1 samples)
- Use least-privilege database users or Microsoft Entra ID / RBAC for applications rather than admin/root credentials; create per-app users or use managed identities. (1 samples)
- Store raw embeddings as a binary-efficient float32 representation rather than JSON strings and ensure drivers/servers preserve numeric precision needed for vector search. (1 samples)
- If a third-party embedder is temporarily unavailable, allow ingestion to persist feature documents without embeddings and flag them for later enrichment/backfill rather than blocking ingestion. (1 samples)
- Use explain() (and explain('executionStats')) and index statistics to verify that production queries use the intended index and that sorts are covered by the index (no blocking SORT stage). (1 samples)
- Create indexes on empty collections or build them in the background / during low-traffic windows; unique indexes block reads/writes and should be created on empty collections. (1 samples)
- Choose and be explicit about write durability/consistency settings appropriate to your workload, but verify how Azure DocumentDB maps or supports MongoDB writeConcern before relying on non-default settings. (1 samples)
- Monitor cluster metrics (CPU, memory, IOPS, connection saturation), watch for throttling during bulk ingestion, and plan capacity (or autoscale) while implementing retry/backoff for rate-limit responses. (1 samples)
- Use explain() and index statistics after seeding data to confirm compound indexes are used and there are no collection scans for the queries your API executes. (1 samples)
- Instrument and monitor cluster and query metrics and capture slow query traces; use explain() to validate important queries. (1 samples)
- Use Microsoft Entra ID (OIDC) and Azure RBAC or native database users as appropriate; support for Entra ID lets you avoid storing long-lived DB passwords and enables least-privilege access control. (1 samples)
- Avoid multi-document transactions for common read-heavy vector-search paths; prefer single-document atomic operations and optimistic concurrency where possible. (1 samples)
- Monitor query performance and index usage (use explain('executionStats') or MCP explain_operation and ensure geospatial queries use a 2dsphere index rather than scanning the collection). (1 samples)
- When using Mongoose in production, avoid enabling autoIndex; prefer controlled index builds as part of deployment. (1 samples)
- Validate and sanitize latitude/longitude input ranges (lat between -90 and 90, lon between -180 and 180) and enforce a reasonable maximum radius for geospatial queries to prevent abusive or expensive queries. (1 samples)
- Connection settings (timeouts, retry options, TLS) follow the service's recommendation for the chosen driver. (1 samples)
- Vector indexes use a type and parameters that suit the data size, with the embedding dimensions matching the model. (1 samples)
- The sample runs with the commands in the README. (1 samples)
- Check cluster tier and service version for DiskANN support before index creation, failing clearly without substituting another index. (1 samples)
- Do not automatically import Amazon DocumentDB's retryWrites=false workaround; Azure migration guidance says remove it while Azure vector/native quickstarts use false. (1 samples)
- Use consistent embedding model/preprocessing and reject mismatched dimensions and nonfinite vector elements. (1 samples)
- Store one embedding-bearing document per product and purchase transactions separately, rather than treating every order as a searchable product. (1 samples)
- Overfetch candidates, exclude the input and duplicate IDs, apply co-purchase/session reranking, then select five. (1 samples)
- Validate URI configuration, await a bounded connect/ping, and distinguish missing, malformed, authentication and connectivity failures without exposing credentials. (1 samples)
- Use a read-only runtime identity and a separate privileged ingestion/setup identity with minimum supported permissions. (1 samples)
- Set finite server-selection, connect and wait-queue timeouts and maxTimeMS on expensive queries. (1 samples)
- Use deterministic order IDs and updateOne/$setOnInsert upserts for repeatable transaction seeding. (1 samples)
- Index the order-item product path and verify containing-order lookup uses it with explain. (1 samples)
- Match containing orders first and count each companion product at most once per order. (1 samples)
- Project only ranking/explanation fields and exclude embeddings and raw orders from responses and LLM context. (1 samples)
- Use authenticated ownership or unguessable session IDs and bound memory history and lifetime. (1 samples)
- Ground explanations in retrieved attributes/counts/session preferences and validate returned IDs against ranked candidates. (1 samples)
- Use idempotent setup comparing existing index definitions and verify vector readiness before recommendations. (1 samples)
- Persist model identity/version and source-content hash, regenerating embeddings when source content or model changes. (1 samples)
- Use private endpoints or explicit firewall allowlists rather than unrestricted public access. (1 samples)
- Resolve or explicitly assess reported dependency vulnerabilities before publication. (1 samples)

## Decisions made with no source (`model_default`)

- **01-fraud-detection-with-vector-similarity** D003: I chose the Python pymongo driver (pymongo>=4.4,<5.0) for connecting to Azure DocumentDB. - pymongo is the officially supported MongoDB driver for Python and is compatible with the DocumentDB MongoDB wire protocol; the driver aligns with the chosen language.
- **01-fraud-detection-with-vector-similarity** D004: I chose sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (local) with 384 dimensions for embeddings. - Using a local sentence-transformers model avoids requiring cloud API keys in the sample and keeps the example reproducible for developers; 384 dims are standard for that model and fit small demo indexes.
- **01-fraud-detection-with-vector-similarity** D007: I chose deterministic, template-based explanations for the LangChain agent when an LLM key is not provided; if OPENAI_API_KEY is present the sample can optionally use OpenAI for richer explanations. - Keeps the sample runnable without cloud LLM keys and still demonstrates LangChain tooling; provides an optional LLM path for users who do have API keys.
- **01-patient-record-summarizer-rag** D003: I chose pymongo (sync) as the Mongo-compatible Python driver, pinned to >=4.3 in requirements.txt. - Synchronous pymongo is simpler for a small sample script and aligns with documentdb-driver guidance about singleton MongoClient. Also the environment uses simple scripts and verification rather than async servers.
- **02-ai-powered-product-recommendations** D005: I chose to persist agent session memory in a DocumentDB collection named 'sessions' in the sample_retail_02 database. - Persisting sessions in DocumentDB makes per-session personalization durable across process restarts and demonstrates using DocumentDB for session storage, which is instructive for sample readers.
- **02-clinical-trial-matching-orchestration** D003: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small with 1536 dimensions for embeddings and environment variable OPENAI_API_KEY to provide the key. - OpenAI embeddings are widely used by LangChain examples and are simple to wire in; 1536 dims is a common choice and supported by 'text-embedding-3-small'.
- **02-clinical-trial-matching-orchestration** D004: I chose OpenAI chat model 'gpt-3.5-turbo' (via OPENAI_API_KEY) for the eligibility and consent agents. - gpt-3.5-turbo is broadly available, lower-cost for samples, and supported by LangChain; it is adequate for small summarization and rationale tasks in a sample app.
- **02-clinical-trial-matching-orchestration** D008: I chose to allow the seed script to run without OPENAI_API_KEY by generating random normalized vectors of dimension 1536 when the key is not present. - This enables the sample to be seeded and verified in environments where an embedding API key is not available while preserving the ability to produce real embeddings when the key is provided.
- **02-real-time-event-stream-ingestion** D003: I chose the official npm 'mongodb' driver version ^5.10.0. - Driver v5.x is stable, supports MongoDB 7 features used (bulkWrite, change streams), and is compatible with Node 24; exact recommended version was not provided in docs so I used a conservative recent 5.x pin.
- **02-real-time-event-stream-ingestion** D008: I chose a default unordered-bulk batch size of 1000, configurable via EVENTS_BATCH_SIZE env var. - 1000 balances throughput vs memory and is a reasonable default for a sample; exposing env var lets users tune it for their cluster tier (e.g., M30).
- **02-swift-message-parser-store** D006: I chose to pin specific recent package versions in requirements.txt (Beanie, Motor, PyMongo, FastAPI, Uvicorn, python-dotenv) - Pinning reduces surprises during pip install and helps reproducibility; versions chosen are recent and likely compatible. If compatibility issues arise, they will be reported in verification.
- **02-swift-message-parser-store** D009: I chose to create explicit indexes programmatically at app startup using the connected Motor/PyMongo client - Explicitly creating indexes makes the sample behaviour clear and ensures the compound and unique indexes are created with intended options; it's easy to verify via MCP tools.
- **02-swift-message-parser-store** D011: I chose Python 3 (CPython 3.13) with FastAPI for an async web sample. - Python is requested in the sample prompt and Beanie (Python ODM) is specified; FastAPI provides an async HTTP surface suitable for ingest and aggregation endpoints.
- **02-swift-message-parser-store** D012: I chose to use Beanie ODM for models and Motor (async) as the underlying client for bulk writes, while initializing Beanie for model validation. - Beanie provides ODM features requested; Motor is commonly used with Beanie and supports async bulk insert via insert_many; documentdb-connection notes PyMongo Async available but Motor is acceptable per prompt assumptions and installed skills.
- **02-swift-message-parser-store** D015: I chose to include an idempotent create_indexes.py script that creates a unique index on message_id and a compound index on (message_type, value_date) and that the seed script will call it. Index creation uses Motor driver. - Indexing improves aggregation performance; an idempotent script is safe to run repeatedly and aligns with sample reproducibility.
- **02-swift-message-parser-store** D016: I chose to implement a simple regex-based parser that extracts common SWIFT tags (:20:, :32A:, :50K:, :59:, etc.) and to document the simplified assumptions and that this is not a complete SWIFT parser. - A full SWIFT parser is out of scope for the sample; a lightweight parser demonstrates ingestion/parsing and keeps sample simple and self-contained.
- **02-swift-message-parser-store** D017: I chose Motor insert_many for bulk writes after Beanie validation. - Motor insert_many offers better throughput for batches; Beanie will be used to validate/construct documents before bulk insert.
- **02-transaction-categorization-agent** D004: I chose OpenAI (environment variable OPENAI_API_KEY) as the LLM provider for LangChain. - OpenAI is widely available, LangChain has direct adapters, and it's simplest for the sample. The README will document required env vars.
- **03-core-banking-transaction-ledger** D005: I chose to expose a small REST API (POST /transactions, GET /accounts/:id) plus an npm-run seed CLI script. - REST API demonstrates atomic posting and retrieval; seed script allows idempotent sample data loading; matches reviewer assumptions and is easy to run manually.
- **03-core-banking-transaction-ledger** D006: I chose to create the unique index on transactions.transactionRef and a compound index {accountId:1, createdAt:-1}. - Unique index enforces idempotency; compound index supports recent-activity queries used by GET /accounts/:id.
- **03-core-banking-transaction-ledger** D011: I chose Mongoose 7.x (pin to 7.9.3). - Mongoose 7.x is stable, widely used and compatible with modern Node; no Node-24-specific authoritative version was available so I selected 7.x and will pin an explicit version in package.json.
- **03-medication-interaction-checker** D003: I chose Mongoose 7.x (e.g., ^7.3.0) as the driver version. - Mongoose 7.x is compatible with Node 24 and matches the prompt-review assumption; it supports modern features and is widely used.
- **03-medication-interaction-checker** D005: I chose to normalize each drug pair into a single sorted key `normalizedPair` (e.g. 'aspirin|ibuprofen') and index that field for efficient pair lookups. - A single normalized key simplifies queries for pairwise interactions and allows a single equality index lookup; it also avoids needing OR queries on drugA/drugB permutations.
- **03-medication-interaction-checker** D006: I chose to create a single-field index on `normalizedPair` and additional single-field indexes on `drugA` and `drugB`. - Index on normalizedPair makes pair lookups efficient; drugA/drugB indexes help other query patterns and improve seed/upsert performance.
- **03-medication-interaction-checker** D007: I chose OpenAI as the LLM provider and will read the API key from OPENAI_API_KEY at runtime. - OpenAI is widely available and supported by LangChain; using OPENAI_API_KEY is a common convention and matches many examples.
- **03-medication-interaction-checker** D008: I chose to implement an HTTP JSON API (Express) with POST /check-interactions and a CLI script that invokes the same logic for ad-hoc runs. - An Express API is simple to run locally and easy to test; CLI helper is convenient for quick demonstrations without making HTTP calls.
- **03-medication-interaction-checker** D009: I chose to provide an idempotent seed script that upserts ~12 example interaction documents by normalizedPair to the `sample_healthcare_03` database. - This gives enough realistic sample data to demonstrate pairwise queries without being large; upsert avoids duplicates on repeated runs.
- **03-medication-interaction-checker** D010: I chose to run unit tests after installing dependencies and before seeding the live cluster to validate local logic and avoid accidental DB writes during test run. - Tests exercise local heuristic LLM fallback and ensure code loads; safer to run tests before seeding the live database.
- **03-medication-interaction-checker** D013: I chose to create the required indexes using Mongoose's model.syncIndexes() in a short script because the app uses Mongoose and this keeps index definitions with the schema. - Centralizing index creation in Mongoose keeps schema and indexes in the same place and is idempotent for sample runs.
- **03-next-best-action-multi-agent** D005: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small with 1536 dimensions for embeddings as the default for the sample, documented and used when OPENAI_API_KEY is provided. - 1536 is common for OpenAI embedding models and matches the readiness_report assumption; it's a reasonable default for the sample and easy to document.
- **03-next-best-action-multi-agent** D006: I chose to make LLM usage optional: use OpenAI (model gpt-4o-mini) when OPENAI_API_KEY is provided; otherwise run deterministic heuristic agents so the sample runs without LLM credentials. - This keeps the sample runnable without paid API keys yet shows how to integrate LangChain when keys are available.
- **03-next-best-action-multi-agent** D015: I chose to design agents to run deterministically without requiring an LLM, while supporting optional OpenAI LLM/embeddings when OPENAI_API_KEY is set. - Ensures the sample runs in environments without API keys while allowing extension to LLM-based agents when keys are available.
- **03-next-best-action-multi-agent** D017: I chose to install Python dependencies from requirements.txt using pip. - To run seed script, demo and tests we need dependencies installed in the virtualenv.
- **03-portfolio-tracker-with-live-alerts** D003: I chose Mongoose 7.x (latest 7.x release) for the sample, e.g., mongoose@7.6.0. - Mongoose 7.x is current, supported on Node 24, and compatible with MongoDB 7.x; it pairs with the modern Node.js driver and simplifies schema definitions for the sample.
- **03-portfolio-tracker-with-live-alerts** D010: I chose not to implement sharding in this sample (single-shard collections). - Sample dataset is small and the environment's cluster is pre-provisioned; sharding is an advanced production technique outside scope of this teaching sample.
- **03-portfolio-tracker-with-live-alerts** D011: I chose a conservative sample policy: single HTTP POST attempt to the webhook and log failures (no durable retry queue). - Keeps the sample simple and focused on change streams and aggregation; production apps should implement durable retries—this is noted in README.
- **04-customer-journey-mapper** D007: I chose to insert 1000 synthetic touchpoint events across 10 customers in the seed script. - 1000 is in the middle of the suggested range: large enough to demonstrate aggregation behavior but small enough to keep seed and runtime quick.
- **04-earnings-call-rag-assistant** D005: I chose to use sentence-transformers model 'all-MiniLM-L6-v2' producing 384-dimensional embeddings for the sample. - Using a local embedding model avoids requiring user's cloud API keys and simplifies sample verification. 384 dims is common for miniLM and inexpensive to compute for the demo.
- **04-earnings-call-rag-assistant** D008: I chose to include a small plaintext sample transcript (samples/earnings_call_sample.txt) in the repo and document how to provide real PDFs. The ingest script supports plain-text files and PDFs if provided by the user. - Including a small text transcript avoids bundling copyrighted/proprietary earnings-call PDFs but still lets the sample run end-to-end. The README documents how to use real PDFs.
- **04-earnings-call-rag-assistant** D009: I chose to use OpenAI (via OPENAI_API_KEY) for answer synthesis if the key is present; otherwise, the sample falls back to a deterministic context-aggregation summarizer that concatenates retrieved passages with citations. - This keeps the sample runnable without requiring an API key while showing how to integrate a real LLM via LlamaIndex/LLMPredictor when available.
- **04-regulatory-reporting-orchestration** D007: I chose to create supporting indexes: transactions: compound {reporting_period:1, date:1} and single-field index on account_id; workflow_runs: index on run_id and start_ts. - These indexes optimize the common query patterns the sample will run (filter by reporting_period and sort by date, lookup by account_id, and query workflow runs by run_id/time).
- **04-regulatory-reporting-orchestration** D008: I chose to make LLM and embedding provider configurable via environment variables and default to OpenAI text-embedding-3-small (1536 dims) and a chat model named gpt-4o-mini when env variables are not provided. - OpenAI models are commonly available to developers, LangChain supports them well, and making them configurable follows the prompt's assumption and safety guidelines.
- **04-regulatory-reporting-orchestration** D009: I chose to implement the orchestration with small LangChain-driven agents encapsulated as Python functions (extraction uses DB queries, validation uses deterministic rules + optional LLM summary, filing uses LLM for formatting), rather than a full Agent framework. - This keeps the sample accessible, shows LangChain integration, and makes the pipeline deterministic where needed while using LLMs for human-friendly summaries/formatting.
- **04-regulatory-reporting-orchestration** D010: I chose not to use vector search or create vector indexes for this sample; the pipeline demonstrates multi-collection extraction, validation rules and filing without vector capabilities. - Avoids adding complexity and external model-dimension coupling; keeps the sample focused on orchestration, validation, and workflow logging.
- **04-regulatory-reporting-orchestration** D017: I chose a synthetic data model with collections: accounts, transactions, positions, and workflow_runs (for orchestrator logs). - The regulatory schema was unspecified; a small synthetic schema covers the sample's goals and keeps the example self-contained while meeting acceptance criteria.
- **05-appointment-scheduling-assistant** D005: I chose OpenAI (OPENAI_API_KEY) as the LLM provider for the sample. - OpenAI is commonly available to developers and works with LangChain; choosing OpenAI keeps the sample familiar. The sample will read OPENAI_API_KEY from env and fall back to a no-LLM mode if unset.
- **05-appointment-scheduling-assistant** D006: I chose in-memory LangChain ConversationBufferMemory for agent memory. - In-memory memory keeps the sample simple to run locally and demonstrates the conversational behavior (e.g., 'move it an hour later'); persistent memory can be added later.
- **05-appointment-scheduling-assistant** D009: I chose OpenAI (via OPENAI_API_KEY) as the chat model provider for the LangChain agent. - OpenAI is widely available for samples, has a stable LangChain integration, and is easy for readers to configure via OPENAI_API_KEY. I will document required env vars in .env.example.
- **05-appointment-scheduling-assistant** D010: I chose in-memory BufferMemory for conversation memory in this sample. - In-memory memory keeps the sample small and simple; it demonstrates the LangChain memory concept so users can extend to persistent stores in their projects. I'll document this limitation in README.
- **05-appointment-scheduling-assistant** D011: I chose two collections: 'slots' and 'appointments' in database 'sample_healthcare_05' with the schema described in the prompt review assumptions. - Two collections map clearly to the domain: slots are availability resources and appointments reference slots; this simplifies queries and demonstrates atomic updates across documents.
- **05-appointment-scheduling-assistant** D012: I chose a compound index { providerId: 1, start: 1, status: 1 } on the slots collection. - This compound index follows the ESR pattern (equality providerId → range start → filter status) and supports the sample queries efficiently.
- **05-branch-performance-analytics** D006: I chose simple lexical retrieval ($search not implemented) using regex/text match and a LangChain-style tool that composes matched summaries and calls OpenAI chat—vector search not implemented in code to keep sample minimal and avoid dimension mismatch risk. - This reduces infra complexity while still demonstrating NL tool over cached summaries and is safe when OPENAI_API_KEY is missing; documentation notes how to add vector search later.
- **05-branch-performance-analytics** D010: I chose to iterate distinct branchIds and run a per-branch $facet aggregation, then upsert the summary per branch. - Per-branch $facet simplifies computing multiple discrete facets (totals and productMix) and avoids complex $group/$push reshaping; performance is acceptable for sample sizes and keeps pipeline easier to read.
- **05-churn-prediction-pipeline** D007: I chose OpenAI 'text-embedding-3-small' (1536 dimensions) for embeddings and to document required env vars OPENAI_API_KEY and OPENAI_EMBEDDING_MODEL. - text-embedding-3-small is cost-effective for a sample app, widely available, and 1536 dims is a common dimensionality; embedding dimensionality must match vector index dimensions.
- **05-churn-prediction-pipeline** D010: I chose to seed 200 synthetic customers and associated transactions for the sample. - Keeps runtime and storage small while still demonstrating aggregation and vector search behavior; easier for local runs and CI.
- **05-kyc-document-ingestion-pipeline** D004: I chose gpt-4o-mini as the OpenAI model for structured extraction in this sample. - gpt-4o-mini balances capability and cost for extraction tasks and is commonly available; the sample focuses on JSON-extraction prompts rather than long conversational context.
- **05-kyc-document-ingestion-pipeline** D005: I chose to upsert records by government_id (stable unique identifier) and create a unique index on government_id. - KYC documents often include government-issued ID numbers as stable identifiers; using government_id makes sample idempotent and realistic for deduplication.
- **05-shopping-cart-with-real-time-inventory** D005: I chose to use Mongoose optimisticConcurrency (versionKey __v) and explicit retry loop for transaction conflicts. - Using Mongoose built-in optimisticConcurrency keeps code concise and matches prompt requirement; add retry loop to surface clear errors when stock exhausted.
- **05-shopping-cart-with-real-time-inventory** D006: I chose to create minimal, practical indexes: index on Inventory.sku and Cart.userId. - Keep sample small while demonstrating index creation; SKU and userId cover common lookup patterns used in sample.
