- **06-credit-risk-multi-agent-scorer** D010: I chose not to use vector/embeddings in this sample. - Removing vector search keeps the sample focused, avoids choosing an embedding model and configuring vector indexes, and simplifies verification.
- **06-omnichannel-interaction-history** D003: I chose Mongoose ^7 (latest 7.x) as the dependency constraint. - Mongoose 7.x supports modern Node.js and MongoDB server versions; readiness report flagged missing exact recommended version, so choose 7.x which is current and compatible.
- **06-order-history-timeline-agent** D005: I chose a single-shard collection named 'orders' in the database 'sample_retail_06' (no sharding). - Sample app is lightweight and the prompt's run environment implies a ready-to-use cluster; sharding is unnecessary for the demo and would complicate the sample.
- **06-order-history-timeline-agent** D008: I chose OpenAI for the sample (chat + embeddings) and will read API key from OPENAI_API_KEY environment variable. Use text-embedding-3-small for embeddings (1536 dims). - OpenAI is commonly available and integrates easily with LangChain; embedding dims are documented for text-embedding-3-small and match vector index guidance.
- **06-symptom-triage-chatbot-rag** D008: I chose to include a small synthetic, hand-crafted corpus of guideline sections (20 items) created for the sample and provided via an idempotent seed script. - Using synthetic content avoids licensing and PHI issues, keeps sample self-contained and safe for demonstration/testing.
- **07-customer-review-sentiment-pipeline** D005: I chose to use the public OpenAI API with OPENAI_API_KEY environment variable. - Public OpenAI is broadly available and simple to demonstrate; the sample will document this requirement in .env.example. If the user prefers Azure OpenAI they can adapt the code later.
- **07-customer-review-sentiment-pipeline** D006: I chose gpt-3.5-turbo as the model for classification and theme extraction. - Lower cost vs gpt-4 for a sample dataset; demonstrates pattern without incurring high usage. The code will accept a MODEL env var to change this.
- **07-customer-review-sentiment-pipeline** D007: I chose a bounded concurrency semaphore of 8 for OpenAI calls. - Small dataset and typical rate limits — 8 concurrent requests balances speed and risk of hitting rate limits; it's configurable in code.
- **07-customer-review-sentiment-pipeline** D013: I chose Python (async) for the sample and targeted a modern 3.11+ runtime but wrote code compatible with the available Python on the machine (3.13 as reported). - The prompt requested an async Python pipeline; the environment already has Python and is compatible with 3.11+; code will be compatible across 3.11–3.13.
- **07-customer-review-sentiment-pipeline** D016: I chose bounded concurrency for OpenAI calls using asyncio.Semaphore with a default concurrency of 5. - Limits rate of outbound LLM requests to be safe and avoid exhausting local resources or API rate limits.
- **07-lab-results-aggregator** D005: I chose to store demo reference ranges as a small in-code dictionary with the option to extend to a DB collection later. - For a compact sample it's simplest and clear to hard-code demo reference ranges; it keeps the sample self-contained and easy to run. The README documents how to replace with a DB-backed or external source.
- **07-mortgage-application-multi-agent** D003: I chose the official MongoDB Node.js driver 'mongodb' at version '^5.0.0'. - Driver v5.x is compatible with Node.js 24 and aligns with documentdb-driver skill guidance (singleton MongoClient).
- **07-mortgage-application-multi-agent** D006: I chose not to create a shard key; collections will be single-shard for this sample. - Simpler for a small demo dataset and aligns with the review assumptions.
- **07-mortgage-application-multi-agent** D007: I chose OpenAI for the embedding and chat models, with embedding model 'text-embedding-3-small' (1536 dims) and a chat model placeholder 'gpt-4o-mini' for agents. - OpenAI is commonly used with LangChain; explicit model and dims allow creating indexes and placeholder environment variables. The sample will fallback to deterministic mocks if credentials are not provided.
- **07-mortgage-application-multi-agent** D008: I chose Jest for unit testing and simple mocking of DB/LLM. - Jest is a common choice for Node.js projects and supports easy mocking for unit tests; aligns with repository conventions assumed in the prompt review.
- **07-mortgage-application-multi-agent** D009: I chose NOT to create a vector index programmatically in the seed script and instead documented the manual step in README. - Vector index creation requires explicit algorithm choice and may depend on cluster capabilities and embedding dimensions; leaving it manual avoids making unsupported calls on the target cluster. The sample still demonstrates the pipeline without vector search.
- **07-voice-of-customer-sentiment-hub** D004: I chose OpenAI embeddings: model 'text-embedding-3-small' and to use EMBEDDING_DIM=1536 as the expected embedding dimension. - OpenAI embeddings are widely available via LangChain and familiar to many users. 'text-embedding-3-small' is a compact, capable choice appropriate for samples. The exact dimension (1536) is set as a best-effort default and documented so users can change it.
- **07-voice-of-customer-sentiment-hub** D005: I chose gpt-3.5-turbo as the default LLM for extraction prompts and set LLM_MODEL='gpt-3.5-turbo'. - gpt-3.5-turbo balances cost and capability for prompt-engineered extraction tasks in a sample. It's widely available to users and integrates with LangChain easily. Users can override via env vars.
- **07-voice-of-customer-sentiment-hub** D012: I chose OpenAI embeddings (text-embedding-3-small) and set EMBEDDING_MODEL accordingly, but provide a deterministic local fallback when API key is not present. - OpenAI via LangChain is common and simpler for users with keys; a fallback ensures the sample works without cloud keys for verification. The exact embedding dim is not authoritative here so it's recorded as a model_default choice.
- **08-dynamic-segmentation-engine** D005: I chose to implement a LangChain-compatible agent that will use OpenAI (if OPENAI_API_KEY is provided) and fall back to a deterministic rule-based assigner when no API key is available. - This keeps the sample faithful to the prompt (LangChain agent) while allowing the demo to run in environments without an LLM key. Secrets are read from env and not stored in files.
- **08-dynamic-segmentation-engine** D006: I chose to provide both a minimal CLI for one-shot demos and a small FastAPI HTTP endpoint (POST /segment) to demonstrate how the agent can be called from an API. - Provides both an easy-to-run demo (CLI) and a realistic integration point (HTTP API) for users.
- **08-dynamic-segmentation-engine** D007: I chose to create the collections and indexes using pymongo in the seed script (idempotent operations), and then verify them with the documentdb MCP server tools. - Creating indexes from the language SDK is a common developer workflow; MCP will be used for verification only (read-only).
- **08-dynamic-segmentation-engine** D008: I chose not to use vector indexes or embeddings for this sample because the segmentation logic is based on behavioural aggregates (recency, frequency, category affinity) and does not require semantic vectors. - Keeps the sample focused, simpler to run, and avoids needing to choose an embedding model and dimension.
- **08-insurance-pre-auth-multi-agent** D007: I chose OpenAI-compatible environment variables (OPENAI_API_KEY) with default model names CHAT_MODEL='gpt-4o-mini' and EMBEDDING_MODEL='text-embedding-3-small' (1536 dims); fall back to deterministic local logic if no API key is present. - OpenAI is common and easy to configure; fall back ensures tests and demo run without external credentials.
- **08-insurance-pre-auth-multi-agent** D008: I chose a lightweight, LangChain-inspired multi-agent structure implemented with small modules (medicalReviewAgent, insurerCommsAgent) rather than pulling in the full langchain npm dependency. - Keeps the sample small, easy to read, and runnable in constrained environments while demonstrating multi-agent orchestration patterns.
- **08-insurance-pre-auth-multi-agent** D021: I chose to not use embeddings/vector indexes and instead query criteria documents by procedureCode and rely on the LLM (or rule-based fallback) for assessment. - Simpler, avoids choosing embedding model/dimension and vector-index parameters given information gaps; keeps sample focused and runnable without extra configuration.
- **08-insurance-pre-auth-multi-agent** D022: I chose OpenAI (api.openai.com) as the default LLM provider, with model name read from CHAT_MODEL env var and API key from OPENAI_API_KEY. - Matches existing code and is a common default; keeps credentials out of repo and configurable via env.
- **08-interest-rate-scenario-modeler** D004: I chose OpenAI as the LLM provider and gpt-4 as the model for the LangChain agent. - OpenAI is widely available, supported by LangChain, and simple to configure via OPENAI_API_KEY for a sample repository.
- **08-personalized-email-campaign-builder** D003: I chose not to use a LlamaIndex library and instead implement a lightweight agent that injects DocumentDB segment context into an OpenAI prompt (or a deterministic fallback when no API key) to generate subject/body. - LlamaIndex Node libraries are not guaranteed available and would increase dependencies; the sample's core teaching point is context-engineering and DocumentDB tool integration, which can be shown by constructing prompts and embedding segment data directly. This also avoids failing the build if LlamaIndex isn't installed or configured.
- **08-personalized-email-campaign-builder** D005: I chose simple context injection (include purchase_history and preferences directly into the prompt) rather than building a vector index or embedding flow. - Keeping the sample small and robust: embedding dimension and vector index choices are ambiguous and not necessary to show personalized email generation. This avoids creating vector indexes and needing embedding model dimensions.
- **08-personalized-email-campaign-builder** D007: I chose to create simple single-field indexes on segments.segmentId (unique) and campaigns.segmentId (non-unique) to speed lookups and demonstrate index creation. - Simple indexes are useful demonstratively, avoid complexity of vector index dimensions and too-specific index types; they are idempotent and low risk on writes for sample data.
- **08-personalized-email-campaign-builder** D009: I chose to prefer the deterministic fallback by default and only call OpenAI when USE_OPENAI=1 is set in environment (and OPENAI_API_KEY present). - This keeps the sample deterministic and avoids flaky network-dependent failures during automated verification; it still allows real LLM usage when the developer sets USE_OPENAI=1 and provides OPENAI_API_KEY.
- **08-regulatory-compliance-rag-checker** D027: I chose to store embeddings in DocumentDB collections and perform a small-scale brute-force nearest-neighbour search in Python at runtime for this sample. - Brute-force NN is simple, reliable for the small sample dataset (few documents), doesn't depend on cluster-specific vector-index syntax, and keeps the sample runnable for developers. I will still create a text index in the DB and include code/comments showing how to create a DocumentDB vector index (HNSW) with the MCP/create-index pattern for production use.
- **08-regulatory-compliance-rag-checker** D028: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small (1536 dimensions) as the embedding provider and to read OPENAI_API_KEY from the environment. - OpenAI is widely available, simple to use in a sample, and LlamaIndex + OpenAI integration is straightforward. Using text-embedding-3-small (1536d) keeps vectors compact and is suitable for the demo scale.
- **08-regulatory-compliance-rag-checker** D029: I chose to implement the runtime RAG flow using OpenAI (embeddings + ChatCompletion) for reliability and to include a LlamaIndex adapter file as a drop-in example for builders who prefer LlamaIndex. - This keeps the sample runnable in this environment (OpenAI direct API is predictable), while still showing how to wire LlamaIndex. I will document the adapter and how to switch to a full LlamaIndex setup in README. This decision is logged as it affects design and verification steps.
- **09-clinical-notes-de-identification** D003: I chose Beanie 1.x with Motor (AsyncIOMotorClient). - Beanie 1.x + Motor is widely used and compatible with many PyPI releases; it is less risky for a sample and simpler to pin dependencies (motor and pymongo).
- **09-clinical-notes-de-identification** D013: I chose to change the tests to avoid instantiating Beanie Document objects and instead test model structure and Settings statically. - Avoid external DB dependency in unit tests makes tests fast and deterministic.
- **09-crypto-wallet-activity-analyzer** D003: I chose the official PyMongo driver (pymongo) pinned to a recent 4.x release. - PyMongo is the standard synchronous driver and aligns with the documentdb-driver skill guidance about a singleton MongoClient pattern and simplicity for a sample app.
- **09-crypto-wallet-activity-analyzer** D007: I chose a single-shard collection (no explicit shard key) and a simple transactions schema with fields {wallet_address, timestamp, amount, currency, tx_id}. - The sample is small, for demos, and the readiness report suggests single-shard is acceptable. It simplifies the seed and aggregation pipeline.
- **09-inventory-reorder-multi-agent-system** D008: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small with 1536 dimensions for embeddings and to create vector index dimensions matching 1536 if vectors are used. - OpenAI embeddings are common in samples; 1536 dims is a practical default and matches the assumption. This decision fills the gap noted in the readiness report about choosing an embedding model.
- **09-inventory-reorder-multi-agent-system** D009: I chose to implement shared agent memory persisted in a small `agent_memory` collection in the same database to prevent duplicate orders across agent runs. - Persisting memory to the DB demonstrates durability and enables multiple orchestrator runs to see recent POs; it's simple to implement and aligns with the prompt requirement.
- **09-lead-scoring-agent** D003: I chose the official mongodb npm driver mongodb@6.x. - Modern stable release compatible with Node.js 24 and aligns with assumptions and skills guidance to use the mongodb driver and MongoClient singleton.
- **09-lead-scoring-agent** D005: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small (1536 dimensions) for embeddings and to document OPENAI_API_KEY as the env var. - Common, well-supported embedding model with 1536 dims that matches LangChain/OpenAI support; easy for users to supply OPENAI_API_KEY.
- **09-lead-scoring-agent** D006: I chose a default of using OpenAI chat model gpt-4o-mini (or falling back to a deterministic local heuristic when OPENAI_API_KEY is not set). - gpt-4o-mini is a capable, lower-cost chat model for reasoning; sample must run in environments without API keys so fallback deterministic scoring is provided for verification.
- **09-lead-scoring-agent** D007: I chose to create only standard (non-vector) indexes in the seed script for robust verification here, and include commented/example code and README notes for creating a vector index (cosmosSearch) matching 1536 dims for users with vector-capable clusters. - Vector index creation can fail or require cluster-specific permissions/commands; to ensure the seed runs against the existing cluster reliably, create conventional indexes and document vector-index steps for users to run manually if desired.
- **09-lead-scoring-agent** D011: I chose the official mongodb npm driver, version ^6.10.0 (mongodb@6.x as declared in package.json). - Official driver is recommended in DocumentDB driver skill and is compatible with Node 24; sample already lists mongodb@6.10.0.
- **09-lead-scoring-agent** D014: I chose the data model: database 'sample_customer_360_09' with collections 'leads', 'activities', 'companies', 'engagement_signals'. - These collections map to the prompt's requirements (lead document, activity history, firmographics, engagement signals) and are simple for a demo.
- **09-lead-scoring-agent** D016: I chose to use OpenAI chat model gpt-4o-mini (configurable via OPENAI_MODEL env var) for scoring reasoning calls. - Balances capability and cost for a sample; make model configurable through env var so users can swap to gpt-4 if desired.
- **09-lead-scoring-agent** D018: I chose to provide an Express HTTP API with POST /score-lead and a CLI script entrypoint (npm run score) that calls the same scoring logic. - Provides both programmatic and CLI access for demos and CI/testing.
- **09-lead-scoring-agent** D025: I chose to avoid vector embeddings and vector indexes in this sample and instead reason directly over retrieved signals with an LLM. - Simpler, avoids needing an embedding provider and creating vector indexes; keeps sample focused on agent tooling and DocumentDB data access.
- **09-trade-settlement-tracker** D003: I chose Mongoose 7.x pinned at 7.14.0 - Mongoose 7.x is current major release and compatible with Node 24; pining to 7.14.0 balances stability and currency (minor versions can be updated later).
- **09-trade-settlement-tracker** D006: I chose to create a compound index {assetClass:1, counterparty:1, currentState:1} plus single-field indexes on currentState - Aggregation will group by assetClass and counterparty and filter by currentState; a compound index in ESR order is appropriate; also create single-field index on currentState for simple filters.
- **09-trade-settlement-tracker** D011: I chose to retry the insert once using the documentdb MCP server - Transient MCP server or permission issue might have caused the failure; retry once to see if it succeeds.
- **10-drug-formulary-semantic-search** D002: I chose Python 3 with FastAPI (uvicorn) as the web framework. - FastAPI is lightweight, async-friendly, and integrates well with uvicorn and LlamaIndex; it's suitable for a small sample service.
- **10-drug-formulary-semantic-search** D003: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small with 1536 dimensions for embeddings. - OpenAI embeddings are widely available, and 1536 is a common dimension size used in examples; this keeps the sample simple and compatible with LlamaIndex.
- **10-expense-report-auto-categorizer** D005: I chose to make OPENAI_API_KEY optional and implement a deterministic rule-based fallback categorizer if an API key is not present. - This keeps the sample runnable in environments without LLM credentials while still supporting LLM-driven behavior when keys are provided.
- **10-expense-report-auto-categorizer** D007: I chose to keep agent memory in-process (LangChain ConversationBufferMemory) but also persist categorization decisions per submitter in a 'decisions' collection for cross-session consistency. - This provides immediate session memory for the agent and durable storage of decisions for reuse across sessions without needing a complex memory-DB integration.
- **10-expense-report-auto-categorizer** D017: I chose to use constrained version ranges in requirements (e.g., beanie>=1.19,<2, motor>=3.1,<4, pymongo>=4.3,<5, langchain>=0.0.200,<1) rather than pinning exact micro versions. - Avoids failing installs due to too-strict pins while still constraining to compatible generations; exact pins couldn't be confidently chosen from available docs.
- **10-expense-report-auto-categorizer** D018: I chose Motor (motor.motor_asyncio.AsyncIOMotorClient) as the Mongo driver to use with Beanie in this sample. - Beanie integrates directly with Motor; while the documentdb-connection skill recommends PyMongo's AsyncMongoClient for new projects, changing to AsyncMongoClient would require verifying Beanie compatibility. To keep the sample implementable and simple, Motor is used.
- **10-expense-report-auto-categorizer** D019: I chose to implement agent memory as an in-session in-memory mapping (merchant -> category) and to include a deterministic rule-based fallback when OPENAI_API_KEY is not provided (so the demo can run without external AI). - This keeps the sample runnable in the developer environment without exposing secrets while still demonstrating how to wire LangChain in when API key is available. It also meets the prompt requirement of consistent decisions across a session.
- **10-multi-tenant-storefront-saas** D003: I chose Mongoose 7.x (specifically ^7.4.0) as the driver version to depend on in package.json. - Mongoose 7.x is compatible with Node 24 and was the prompt-review assumption; it avoids potential breaking changes of major newer versions while staying modern.
- **10-multi-tenant-storefront-saas** D006: I chose not to set a shard key in this sample and only implement tenantId as a required field with compound indexes (tenantId, sku) and (tenantId, createdAt). - Sharding would require cluster-level operations and provisioning; for a sample demonstrating repository-layer tenant isolation it's sufficient to enforce tenantId and create compound indexes as requested.
- **10-support-ticket-rag-assistant** D004: I chose gpt-4o-mini as the default chat/generation model for concise suggestions. - gpt-4o-mini is a lower-cost, fast conversational model suitable for concise answer generation in a sample app.
- **10-support-ticket-rag-assistant** D016: I chose the pinned versions in requirements.txt: pymongo==4.5.1, Flask==2.3.2, python-dotenv==1.0.0, openai==0.28.1, llama-index==0.9.2, requests==2.31.0. - Pinned versions reduce the chance of incompatibilities during installation and verification in this run environment.
- **10-support-ticket-rag-assistant** D020: I chose to compute deterministic pseudo-embeddings locally to populate the 'embedding' field for seed documents (keeps sample runnable without OPENAI_API_KEY). - Avoid requiring API key for initial verification; deterministic embeddings allow retrieval flow to be exercised.
- **10-wealth-management-rag-portal** D003: I chose Python 3.11 - Many libraries used (llama_index, deps) are most commonly tested on 3.11; choosing 3.11 maximizes compatibility while remaining recent.
- **10-wealth-management-rag-portal** D004: I chose pymongo (MongoDB driver) pinned to 'pymongo>=4.3,<5' - PyMongo 4.x supports MongoDB 7.0 wire protocol and is recommended by the documentdb-driver skill for Azure DocumentDB.
- **10-wealth-management-rag-portal** D012: I chose to target Python 3.11 for compatibility but will run with the system Python available at runtime (3.13) if necessary. - LlamaIndex and related ecosystem are commonly validated on Python 3.11; using 3.11 as target ensures better compatibility while allowing the provided system Python to execute the sample.
- **10-wealth-management-rag-portal** D013: I chose pymongo>=4.3,<5 as the MongoDB driver dependency. - MongoDB wire protocol 7.0 requires modern pymongo 4.x; pinning <5 avoids potential incompatible major upgrades.
- **10-wealth-management-rag-portal** D016: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small with 1536 dimensions as the default embedding model for the sample, with a fallback to synthetic random normalized vectors when OPENAI_API_KEY is not provided. - Using a well-known public model avoids vendor lock for examples; providing a fallback keeps the sample runnable without an API key while ensuring vector dimensions match the index.
- **10-wealth-management-rag-portal** D017: I chose OpenAI gpt-4o-mini as the default chat/completion model for the RAG agent in examples and prompts, configurable via environment variables. - gpt-4o-mini is a cost-effective, capable chat model suitable for examples; making it configurable keeps the sample flexible for other providers.
- **11-care-gap-identification-pipeline** D007: I chose OpenAI provider with a mainstream chat model (gpt-3.5-turbo) and embeddings (text-embedding-3-small). - gpt-3.5-turbo is broadly available and less likely to be blocked in this environment than specialty models; text-embedding-3-small provides stable embeddings if needed. This choice reduces friction for users running the sample.
- **11-care-gap-identification-pipeline** D010: I chose PyMongo (pymongo >=4.0) as the MongoDB driver and will follow the documentdb-driver guidance (singleton MongoClient). - PyMongo is the standard Python MongoDB driver and matches the documentdb-driver skill guidance.
- **11-care-gap-identification-pipeline** D012: I chose OpenAI as the default LLM provider but will implement a deterministic fallback mode when OPENAI_API_KEY is not set so the sample runs without external LLM credentials. - Many users have OPENAI_API_KEY; adding a fallback ensures the sample can be run in environments without keys for verification.
- **11-care-gap-identification-pipeline** D013: I chose not to require vector indexes or embeddings for this sample; the agent will use aggregation pipelines and LLM reasoning (or a deterministic fallback) to prioritize and produce talking points. - Simpler to keep sample focused on aggregation tool and prompt-engineering for guidelines; avoids embedding model choice and vector index creation tangles.
- **11-care-gap-identification-pipeline** D014: I chose a simple data model: single 'patients' collection containing patient_id, name, dob, sex, diagnosis_codes, last_visit_date, contact. Seed script will populate synthetic records in database 'sample_healthcare_11'. - Simpler for aggregation pipelines and sample seed; avoids needing to join collections for this sample.
- **11-care-gap-identification-pipeline** D021: I chose two collections: 'patients' and 'visits' (normalized). 'visits' stores historical visit dates and types; 'patients' stores demographics and diagnosis codes. - Normalized visits makes it easier to seed multiple visits per patient and run aggregation pipelines to compute last_visit_date; it's realistic for healthcare data modeling.
- **11-care-gap-identification-pipeline** D022: I chose to not use vector search or embeddings for this initial sample; the agent will run DocumentDB aggregations to identify gaps and then use an LLM for prioritization and talking points. Vector search is optional and documented in README. - Keeps the sample simpler and avoids needing an embedding model and vector index configuration; achieves goals (identify gaps, prioritize, generate talking points).
- **11-care-gap-identification-pipeline** D023: I chose OpenAI as the default LLM provider and 'gpt-4o-mini' as the default reasoning model, configurable via environment variables; the app will gracefully skip live LLM calls if no API key is present. - OpenAI is a common, widely available provider. Choosing a light model reduces cost during tests; but the code will be configurable and skip calls if key absent.
- **11-correspondent-banking-risk-screener** D005: I chose OpenAI embeddings (text-embedding-3-small) with 1536 dimensions by default, configurable via env. - OpenAI embeddings are commonly available and simple to demonstrate; 1536 is the correct dimension for text-embedding-3-small; making it configurable ensures users can change provider in real runs.
- **11-correspondent-banking-risk-screener** D012: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small (1536 dimensions) for embeddings. - OpenAI embeddings are commonly available, simple to use in samples, and 1536 dims matches many LangChain examples and the cluster's vector-index examples; keeps index dimension manageable for demos.
- **11-correspondent-banking-risk-screener** D013: I chose OpenAI gpt-3.5-turbo for generating the plain-language narrative. - gpt-3.5-turbo is broadly available and sufficient for generating short narratives in samples; it reduces chance of requiring special access compared to newer models.
- **11-correspondent-banking-risk-screener** D014: I chose to provide a small FastAPI app with a POST /review endpoint and a CLI demo script. - FastAPI makes the sample easy to run locally and call via curl; also provides a clear API surface for integrations.
- **11-correspondent-banking-risk-screener** D015: I chose to inject synthetic normalized random embeddings into seed documents when OPENAI_API_KEY is not present to allow verification of vector indexes and queries. - OPENAI_API_KEY was not set in the run environment, so to verify vector-index behavior and queries we will generate deterministic random vectors for the sample documents. This keeps secrets out of files and allows the sample to run in CI or a developer machine without an API key.
- **11-market-news-sentiment-orchestration** D004: I chose OpenAI (environment var OPENAI_API_KEY) as the LLM provider. - OpenAI is common, supported by LangChain, and simple to configure for a sample; it keeps the sample minimal and portable.
- **11-market-news-sentiment-orchestration** D005: I chose to scrape Reuters RSS (https://www.reutersagency.com/feed/?best-topics=business-finance) and the Reuters 'Business News' RSS feed for headlines. - Public RSS feeds require no API key and are stable for small demos; Reuters business headlines are representative for finance demo.
- **11-market-news-sentiment-orchestration** D006: I chose a simple Bearer-token secured webhook: POST JSON to WEBHOOK_URL with Authorization: Bearer <WEBHOOK_TOKEN> if provided. - Bearer token is easy to implement and safe-enough for sample apps; avoids HMAC complexity and keeps sample focused on DocumentDB and LangChain.
- **11-market-news-sentiment-orchestration** D009: I chose to implement a lightweight heuristic fallback so the demo can run even without an OpenAI key. - A fallback ensures the sample is runnable for verification and demonstration purposes while clearly documenting the preferred OpenAI option in README and .env.example.
- **11-preference-center-consent-store** D004: I chose a 365-day retention period and will populate retention_expires_at = now + 365 days on writes. - One year is a commonly-used retention window for consent records and is sensible for a sample; will document it in README and make it configurable via env var.
- **11-preference-center-consent-store** D005: I chose to pin dependencies to specific, recent compatible versions in requirements.txt. - Pinned versions improve reproducibility for the sample; I'll pick recent stable releases compatible with Beanie and FastAPI and record this decision.
- **11-preference-center-consent-store** D013: I chose a default retention period of 365 days for consent records in this sample. - One year is a sensible demo default for consent retention and easy to understand; documentable and configurable via environment variable.
- **11-visual-similarity-search-image-embeddings** D003: I chose the Hugging Face checkpoint 'openai/clip-vit-base-patch32' to produce 512-dimensional embeddings. - ViT-B/32 is a standard CLIP that produces 512-dim vectors and is widely available in transformers; keeps compute moderate for sample.
- **11-visual-similarity-search-image-embeddings** D005: I chose to use picsum.photos image URLs as permissive sample images for seeding. - Simpler, no API keys, reproducible images via seed URL.
- **12-abandoned-cart-recovery-orchestration** D003: I chose the official 'mongodb' npm package, pinned to '5.7.0' for Node.js 24 compatibility and to follow the documentdb-driver guidance. - mongodb 5.x is modern and compatible with Node 24 and Azure DocumentDB MongoDB wire protocol; it aligns with the prompt review assumption and documentdb-driver guidance.
- **12-abandoned-cart-recovery-orchestration** D007: I chose OpenAI as the hosted LLM provider (via LangChain/OpenAI integration) and expect OPENAI_API_KEY in environment. - OpenAI is widely available and supported by LangChain; keeps sample simple. Documented in README and .env.example.
- **12-abandoned-cart-recovery-orchestration** D011: I chose the official 'mongodb' npm package, version ^5.9.0 (modern, Node-24-compatible) and will follow the singleton MongoClient pattern from the documentdb-driver skill. - Official driver offers direct MongoDB API and is recommended for samples that show low-level DocumentDB usage and indexing. Mongoose would add complexity and hide driver patterns the skills recommend.
- **12-abandoned-cart-recovery-orchestration** D014: I chose OpenAI (openai.com API) as the LLM provider and to integrate it via LangChain's OpenAI LLM wrapper, with credentials provided by OPENAI_API_KEY in env. - OpenAI is widely supported by LangChain and simplifies sample setup; use environment-based API key to avoid embedding secrets.
- **12-abandoned-cart-recovery-orchestration** D015: I chose to implement pluggable channel adapters with a 'simulated' (console/log) sender by default and provide connectors for Twilio (SMS) and SendGrid (email) that are used only if credentials are present in env; otherwise it falls back to simulation. - This keeps the sample runnable without paid provider credentials, while documenting how to wire real providers in .env.example and README. Tests will mock sending adapters.
- **12-abandoned-cart-recovery-orchestration** D016: I chose a CLI/one-shot script (node src/run.js) that executes the pipeline once per invocation; README will document scheduling (cron/Azure Function). - Simpler for a sample, avoids long-running server in the runner, and matches the prompt's guidance.
- **12-abandoned-cart-recovery-orchestration** D022: I chose the official `mongodb` npm driver version 5.8.0 (mongodb 5.x) and will follow the singleton MongoClient pattern. - mongodb 5.x is stable and compatible with Node 24 and DocumentDB wire protocols; skills recommend using a modern driver and singleton pattern (documentdb-driver).
- **12-abandoned-cart-recovery-orchestration** D023: I chose OpenAI (OPENAI_API_KEY via env) as the hosted LLM provider and will use langchainjs with the OpenAI chat model (gpt-3.5-turbo). - OpenAI is widely used and supported by LangChain JS; environment-based key keeps secrets out of files and matches prompt assumptions.
- **12-abandoned-cart-recovery-orchestration** D024: I chose to implement channel agents that simulate email/SMS sends but include adapter interfaces and `.env.example` placeholders for Twilio and SendGrid so users can wire real providers. - Avoids sending real messages from sample while showing how to integrate real providers; keeps the sample safe for CI and dev runs.
- **12-abandoned-cart-recovery-orchestration** D028: I chose to use dotenv to load runtime environment variables from a .env file in development only, with a .env.example provided. - dotenv is standard and will not commit real secrets; .env.example documents required variables.
- **12-clv-prediction-pipeline** D005: I chose OpenAI gpt-3.5-turbo as the default LLM for the LangChain Chat model. - gpt-3.5-turbo is commonly available and least likely to require elevated permissions; safer for sample. Credentials loaded from OPENAI_API_KEY environment variable.
- **12-clv-prediction-pipeline** D006: I chose to create a multikey index on 'purchases.date' to accelerate aggregations that unwind purchases by date and compute tenure/recency. - An index on purchases.date helps aggregation that filters by date ranges or sorts by purchase date; multikey array index is supported.
- **12-cross-border-payment-audit-trail** D008: I chose to retry documentdb_create_index without passing connection_profile (omit it) on the second attempt. - A second attempt with a slightly different parameter set may succeed; per instructions stop after second attempt if it still fails.
- **12-invoice-matching-reconciliation** D005: I chose ephemeral in-memory session memory stored in the running process (non-persistent) - Simpler for a sample, matches "within a session" wording and avoids adding persistence/extra resources; document as a design choice.
- **12-invoice-matching-reconciliation** D006: I chose OpenAI models: gpt-4o-mini for extraction (chat) and text-embedding-3-small for embeddings - OpenAI is widely used and supported by LangChain; text-embedding-3-small is a reasonable small embedding model for demo; exact dims unknown so noted in report as not verified.
- **12-invoice-matching-reconciliation** D007: I chose to include both LangChain and OpenAI SDK but perform LLM calls via OpenAI SDK for reliability, while exposing a simple LangChain-style agent wrapper - LangChain JS can be unstable across versions; using the OpenAI SDK for direct calls increases reliability while still providing a LangChain-compatible wrapper for demo purposes.
- **12-invoice-matching-reconciliation** D011: I chose to use the OpenAI SDK directly instead of LangChain for simplicity and reliability in this sample. - Direct OpenAI SDK reduces dependency complexity for the sample and keeps extraction and embedding calls explicit; code is structured so LangChain could be added later.
- **12-invoice-matching-reconciliation** D012: I chose OpenAI 'text-embedding-3-small' for embeddings and assume 1536 dimensions. - text-embedding-3-small is a commonly used OpenAI embedding for sample apps; 1536 dims is a plausible dimension (documented for many OpenAI embeddings). This choice will be documented and can be changed by users.
- **13-basel-iii-capital-ratio-calculator** D008: I chose nested capital object with fields capital.cet1 and capital.total to represent capital elements in each position document. - Nested 'capital' groups capital elements logically and makes it extensible for tier entries; it's a common pattern in financial doc schemas.
- **13-basel-iii-capital-ratio-calculator** D009: I chose to rely on the default _id index and not create special indexes because the sample aggregation is a full-collection $group summation. - A full-collection aggregation will scan the collection; adding indexes would not help the $group unless filtered. Simplicity for sample is preferred.
- **13-chronic-disease-risk-predictor** D004: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small as the embedding model with 1536 dimensions. - Provides a good balance of cost and quality; 1536 dims is widely supported and recommended in the assumptions.
- **13-chronic-disease-risk-predictor** D024: I chose OpenAI (api.openai.com) for the LLM provider, configurable via OPENAI_API_KEY. - OpenAI is widely available, supported by LlamaIndex, and straightforward for a sample; code will gracefully fall back to a deterministic template if no API key is present to allow offline runs.
- **13-chronic-disease-risk-predictor** D025: I chose FastAPI for the HTTP API surface (uvicorn-runable). - FastAPI provides easy async endpoints, automatic OpenAPI docs, and works well with uvicorn used in tests and examples.
- **13-chronic-disease-risk-predictor** D032: I chose to integrate LlamaIndex as an optional component: when OPENAI_API_KEY is present, use OpenAI chat via LlamaIndex to generate the personalised narrative; otherwise produce a deterministic template combining retrieved profiles. - Keeps sample faithful to prompt while allowing runs/tests without external LLM calls; documents configuration in README.
- **13-chronic-disease-risk-predictor** D033: I chose the API response to return the generated risk narrative plus the top-k retrieved profiles and their similarity scores. - Helps developers inspect why the agent made recommendations and validates retrieval correctness.
- **13-household-grouping-linkage** D008: I chose @faker-js/faker for synthetic data generation. - Avoid module-not-found and use a maintained faker library compatible with Node 24.
- **13-household-grouping-linkage** D012: I chose Mongoose 7.x (latest 7 series compatible with Node 24). - Mongoose 7.x is current and compatible with Node 24 and offers stable APIs; it matches the run-environment assumptions.
- **13-product-q-a-chatbot-rag** D006: I chose to store product documents in 'products' and chunked text as separate documents in 'product_chunks' collection. - Storing chunks separately eases vector indexing and retrieval; aligns with RAG patterns and sample assumptions.
- **13-product-q-a-chatbot-rag** D008: I chose OpenAI gpt-4o-mini as the default LLM for generation (configurable via environment). - OpenAI is commonly available for LlamaIndex integration; for users without keys the code supports alternative LLM backends later. Keep model name configurable in .env.
- **13-product-q-a-chatbot-rag** D009: I chose to implement a simple CLI REPL chat loop (python app.py) as the primary app surface. - Keeps sample compact and easy to verify in this environment; option to extend with HTTP is noted in README.
- **14-customer-complaint-classifier** D002: I chose to use Beanie ODM with Motor (motor>=3.x) and PyMongo pinned alongside it, because the prompt explicitly requires Beanie; despite documentdb-connection recommending PyMongo's AsyncMongoClient, Beanie depends on Motor. - The prompt's requirement to use Beanie ODM takes priority for this sample; I will follow driver best-practices (singleton client) while using Motor to satisfy Beanie compatibility.
- **14-customer-complaint-classifier** D010: I chose OpenAI provider with 'text-embedding-3-small' (1536 dims) for embeddings and 'gpt-4o-mini' for classification calls made by LangChain. - OpenAI models are widely available and fit the sample's needs; text-embedding-3-small is 1536-dimensional which matches vector index choice; using environment-provided OPENAI_API_KEY keeps secrets out of files.
- **14-dynamic-pricing-engine** D004: I chose OpenAI via LangChain as the primary LLM integration, with a configurable mock fallback when OPENAI_API_KEY is not set (USE_MOCK_LLM env var). - OpenAI + LangChain is a common, well-supported configuration for samples; adding a mock fallback ensures verification can run without a paid API key.
- **14-dynamic-pricing-engine** D009: I chose to make the LLM provider configurable via environment variables and default to OpenAI (OPENAI_API_KEY) and model name from ENV MODEL (default 'gpt-4'). - OpenAI is commonly available and LangChain supports it; making provider configurable keeps sample flexible.
- **14-dynamic-pricing-engine** D015: I chose the synchronous pymongo driver (pymongo >= 4.4) with a singleton MongoClient. - Synchronous pymongo is simpler for the sample and aligns with DocumentDB driver best-practices guidance; singleton MongoClient avoids per-request creation overhead.
- **14-dynamic-pricing-engine** D018: I chose the data model with collections: products, demand_metrics, competitor_prices, margin_rules and simple document shapes described in README. - These collections directly map to the data the pricing agent needs and keep the schema small and readable for a sample.
- **14-dynamic-pricing-engine** D019: I chose to create indexes: products.sku (unique), competitor_prices {sku:1, timestamp:-1}, demand_metrics {sku:1, timestamp:-1}, margin_rules {sku_or_category:1}. - Indexes speed queries the sample uses (recent competitor prices and metrics grouped by SKU) and reflect typical patterns; keep write costs low for a small sample.
- **14-dynamic-pricing-engine** D020: I chose OpenAI (set OPENAI_API_KEY and MODEL env vars) as the default LLM provider for the LangChain agent. - OpenAI is commonly available with LangChain examples; the sample will accept MODEL env var for flexibility. Users can swap in Azure OpenAI by modifying the code/configs.
- **14-dynamic-pricing-engine** D021: I chose not to use embeddings or vector search for this sample; the agent will use structured data queries and an LLM for reasoning only. - Keeping the sample focused on DocumentDB queries and the LangChain tool pattern reduces complexity; vector indexes are available but not necessary for the described pricing logic.
- **14-hyper-personalized-content-engine** D003: I chose the official 'mongodb' Node.js driver version ^5.7.0 and will use a singleton MongoClient. - Driver 5.x is the current stable major compatible with modern Node versions; following documentdb-driver guidance for singleton client avoids connection-pool issues.
- **14-hyper-personalized-content-engine** D005: I chose to expose a minimal HTTP API (Express) with a /search endpoint that accepts customerId and returns rationale + ranked content. - An HTTP API makes the agent easy to call and demonstrates the retrieval + ranking behavior; it matches the prompt's expectation for an agent surface and is easy to test locally.
- **14-hyper-personalized-content-engine** D006: I chose to create a 'vector-hnsw' cosmosSearch index for the sample content collection with dimensions 1536 and similarity 'COS' (cosine). - Small sample dataset (tens/hundreds) — HNSW is simple and fast for local/small-scale samples. Chose 1536 dimensions to match the default embedding model selection (OpenAI text-embedding-3-small).
- **14-hyper-personalized-content-engine** D007: I chose to generate synthetic random normalized embeddings for the seed data instead of calling an external embedding API during seed. - Avoid making network calls to external embedding services during automated verification and avoid requiring secrets. Seed documents will include embeddings with the chosen dimension so vector index and queries can be demonstrated using interaction embeddings as query vectors.
- **14-hyper-personalized-content-engine** D008: I chose not to add an external LlamaIndex Node package and instead implement a small, self-contained retrieval+context-engineering agent in the sample code. - Avoid depending on an unconfirmed third-party package; sample can still show the LlamaIndex-style workflow (profile retrieval, vector search, context engineering) without the external dependency, keeping the sample simple and runnable.
- **14-hyper-personalized-content-engine** D010: I chose to retry documentdb_create_index with an alternative options payload including indexType 'cosmosSearch' and altering keys to be an array form. - A second attempt with an adjusted shape may succeed; if it fails I'll record it and proceed without MCP-created index (the seed data and driver-based queries still work).
- **14-hyper-personalized-content-engine** D011: I chose to implement a runtime fallback: try $search knn, and if it fails (index missing), run a collection scan and perform cosine similarity in the application to return ranked results. - This keeps the sample robust in environments where cosmosSearch index creation via MCP wasn't possible, and it still demonstrates vector-style retrieval.
- **14-hyper-personalized-content-engine** D014: I chose the official 'mongodb' Node.js driver pinned to ^5.7.0 in package.json. - Driver 5.x is modern and compatible with Node 24 and supports MongoClient singleton patterns; pining to ^5.7.0 gives concrete reproducible dependency while being reasonably recent.
- **14-hyper-personalized-content-engine** D015: I chose to use a deterministic 'mock' embedding provider for the sample (EMBEDDING_PROVIDER=mock) with default EMBEDDING_DIM=384, and make embedding/chat provider configurable via env variables. - Avoids requiring external API keys during verification while keeping code pluggable for real providers; 384 dims is compact for local testing and common for small models (e.g., MiniLM family). The README will document how to switch to a real provider and set EMBEDDING_DIM accordingly.
- **14-hyper-personalized-content-engine** D016: I chose to implement retrieval by application-side cosine similarity over stored embeddings for the sample, while still creating a cosmosSearch vector index (vector-hnsw) to demonstrate DocumentDB index creation and to allow users to switch to server-side kNN if desired. - Safer and deterministic for a tiny sample; still demonstrates index creation and documentdb_list_indexes can verify index exists. Keeps sample working without depending on precise $search query syntax which can vary by engine version.
- **14-hyper-personalized-content-engine** D019: I chose to implement a lightweight retrieval+generation agent in the repository instead of depending on a third-party LlamaIndex Node package. - Avoids adding uncertain external dependency and keeps sample focused and easy to run without API keys. The code is modular so teams can replace the mock agent with a real LlamaIndex integration later.
- **14-loan-origination-workflow-orchestration** D006: I chose to implement deterministic/stub agents by default and allow optional real LLM usage if OPENAI_API_KEY is set in the environment. - This keeps the sample runnable and deterministic without external API keys while preserving the LangChain integration path for users who want to enable real LLMs.
- **14-loan-origination-workflow-orchestration** D008: I chose to pin explicit dependency versions in requirements.txt to improve reproducibility. - Pinned versions reduce CI/install breakage for the sample; exact choices are based on commonly-compatible releases.
- **14-telehealth-session-summarizer** D005: I chose to call OpenAI's Chat API directly for the summarisation step and structure the prompt using LlamaIndex-style prompt engineering; the code includes llama-index in requirements for extensibility. - Direct OpenAI API is more predictable for a small sample; we will still follow LlamaIndex prompt-engineering patterns and keep llama-index as an optional dependency. This ensures the sample runs even if llama-index versions differ in the environment.
- **14-telehealth-session-summarizer** D006: I chose gpt-3.5-turbo as the default LLM model for the sample. - gpt-3.5-turbo is broadly available to users, cheaper than gpt-4, and sufficient for structured summarisation in a sample. Using it reduces friction for verification.
- **14-telehealth-session-summarizer** D007: I chose text-embedding-3-small (1536 dimensions) as the embedding model if embeddings are used; for this sample embeddings are optional and vector-index creation will be omitted by default. - text-embedding-3-small balances cost and dimensionality for small demos. But to keep the sample simpler and avoid creating vector indexes and extra cluster writes, embeddings are optional and not created automatically.
- **14-telehealth-session-summarizer** D008: I chose not to create a vector index by default. Instead the sample will create a patientId lookup index and include optional code/comments for creating a vector `cosmosSearch` index if the user wants embeddings. - Avoids adding vector-index complexity and cluster write operations in the default sample run. Keeps verification simpler while still documenting how to add a vector index and what dimensions to use.
- **15-cohort-analysis-backend** D016: I chose to pin versions in requirements.txt (fastapi 0.95.2, pymongo 4.5.0, uvicorn 0.23.2, apscheduler 3.10.1). - Pinning ensures reproducible installs for the sample and matches tested driver versions compatible with MongoDB 7.0
- **15-digital-onboarding-orchestration** D003: I chose Beanie 1.21.x (stable 1.x series) for ODM. - Use a stable 1.x series compatible with Motor and existing docs; avoids major API changes in Beanie 2 if not necessary.
- **15-digital-onboarding-orchestration** D004: I chose to pin specific package versions in requirements.txt (Beanie==1.21.0, motor==3.1.1, pymongo==4.5.0, langchain==0.0.236, openai==1.0.0, python-dotenv==1.0.0, pytest==7.4.0, pytest-asyncio==0.22.0). - Pinned versions improve reproducibility for the sample and tests; pick reasonably recent stable releases.
- **15-digital-onboarding-orchestration** D007: I chose beanie==1.24.1 and motor==3.1.1 pinned in requirements.txt. - Beanie 1.x is the modern stable line; I picked 1.24.1 as a recent 1.x release compatible with Motor 3.x and Python 3.13. This keeps the sample current while avoiding API mismatch.
- **15-financial-advisor-chatbot-rag** D004: I chose to use deterministic mock embeddings (fixed-dimension pseudo-random vectors) in the seed data so the sample is runnable without external API keys; README documents how to swap in real embeddings from OpenAI. - Keeps the sample self-contained and runnable; still models real usage and allows users with API keys to regenerate embeddings.
- **15-financial-advisor-chatbot-rag** D005: I chose to support OpenAI (via OPENAI_API_KEY) for real LLM calls when available, and fall back to a deterministic local mock responder when the key is not set. - Allows the sample to run end-to-end without external keys while enabling real LLM behavior when the user provides credentials. README will explain env vars and how to enable real LLMs.
- **15-financial-advisor-chatbot-rag** D013: I chose to implement agent memory as an in-memory per-session conversation history for this sample. - In-memory memory is sufficient for a sample and keeps the code small and focused; the README will document persistence as a production improvement.
- **15-financial-advisor-chatbot-rag** D017: I chose the mongodb Node.js driver v5.x and will implement the singleton MongoClient pattern. - Driver v5.x is current and the documentdb-driver skill recommends a singleton client for Azure DocumentDB.
- **15-financial-advisor-chatbot-rag** D018: I chose to use OpenAI text-embedding-3-small as the recommended provider (1536 dims) but seed the sample with deterministic, local embeddings when OPENAI_API_KEY is not present. - This keeps the sample runnable without external API keys while documenting how to switch to OpenAI-based embeddings when the user supplies credentials.
- **15-financial-advisor-chatbot-rag** D020: I chose to implement the portfolio tool as a mocked local function/endpoint returning deterministic JSON used by the demo and tests. - Avoids requiring third-party credentials and keeps the sample self-contained while demonstrating the agent-tool interaction.
- **15-financial-advisor-chatbot-rag** D021: I chose the data model: a 'knowledge' collection with documents { _id, title, content, embedding: number[], metadata: {source, id, date, model, dims} }. - This model is simple, compatible with DocumentDB vector index expectations, and records the embedding provenance for reproducibility.
- **15-prescription-refill-agent** D003: I chose Mongoose (mongoose@7) as the ODM/driver for the Node.js sample. - Mongoose simplifies schema definitions and seeding for a small sample app and is familiar to Node.js developers.
- **15-prescription-refill-agent** D006: I chose a simple in-process conversation memory for this sample agent (keeps last medication mention per session). - Simpler for a lightweight sample, easier to understand and safe for demo PHI; production apps should use durable memory stores.
- **15-prescription-refill-agent** D007: I chose to implement a small rule-based agent (with explicit tools) rather than a full LangChain dependency to keep the sample small, readable, and self-contained. - Keeps the sample easier to run in constrained environments and allows deterministic demo runs; document this decision in README so users can replace the agent with LangChain if desired.
- **15-prescription-refill-agent** D011: I chose a DB-backed agent memory stored in collection 'agent_memory'. - DB-backed memory persists across restarts and multiple processes, demonstrating a realistic memory backing and allowing the follow-up utterance 'refill my blood pressure medication' to resolve to prior medication for the patient.
- **15-prescription-refill-agent** D012: I chose to implement a small rule-based agent (no external LLM calls) as the default behavior; the code is structured so an LLM (OpenAI) can be plugged via env var OPENAI_API_KEY. - Avoid requiring the user to provide an API key to run the demo and to keep verification self-contained. The implementation is pluggable so LangChain/OpenAI can be enabled by setting OPENAI_API_KEY in the environment.
- **15-supplier-po-management** D003: I chose Motor as the MongoDB async driver because Beanie requires Motor. - Beanie currently depends on Motor for async integration; to use Beanie as requested we must use Motor despite guidance that PyMongo's async API exists.
- **15-supplier-po-management** D004: I chose to request latest compatible releases (no strict pins) in requirements.txt. - Using latest compatible releases reduces the chance of incompatibility with the runtime here; this is acceptable for a sample app. If a consumer needs strict reproducibility they can pin versions later.
- **15-supplier-po-management** D007: I chose float for PurchaseOrder.total_value in this sample. - Float is simpler for a small sample app and avoids additional Decimal/PyMongo type handling; note in README that production should use Decimal or specialized money types.
- **16-atm-network-status-monitor** D005: I chose to maintain an atm_statuses collection where the app upserts the latest status per ATM and compute the network summary by aggregating that collection. - Using an upsert collection makes summary computation simple and idempotent across restarts and multiple processes; it's suitable for a sample and avoids in-memory drift.
- **16-atm-network-status-monitor** D006: I chose to create indexes: heartbeat collection index on { atmId:1, timestamp:-1 } and an index on { status:1 }; and a TTL index option commented as optional for retention. - These indexes support latest-per-ATM lookups and status-based queries; TTL indexing is useful for sample retention but optional due to write-costs.
- **16-multi-touch-attribution-model** D007: I chose LangChain with OpenAI provider (OPENAI_API_KEY) for the marketer-facing natural language tool. - OpenAI provider is widely used and available in the environment; LangChain integration with OpenAI is straightforward for this sample.
- **16-population-health-dashboard-backend** D003: I chose Python 3.11 as the target minor version for the sample. - 3.11 is a recent stable release with broad compatibility for examples and CI; it matches the prompt review suggestion.
- **16-population-health-dashboard-backend** D005: I chose APScheduler (BackgroundScheduler) as an in-process scheduler to refresh materialized summaries on a schedule. - For a small sample app it's simplest to show scheduling inside the app process; APScheduler is a common lightweight choice that lets developers see refresh behavior without external infra.
- **16-population-health-dashboard-backend** D007: I chose to generate 5,000 synthetic patient records for the seed script. - 5,000 is in the 'few thousand' range—large enough to produce meaningful facet buckets for the sample while remaining quick to insert for testing.
- **16-population-health-dashboard-backend** D009: I chose a default refresh schedule of once per hour for the materialized summaries, with an endpoint to trigger immediate refreshes. - Hourly refresh balances currency with cost and is a reasonable default for population-health dashboards; making it configurable allows users to adjust later.
- **17-ai-survey-response-analyzer** D015: I chose to append the repository root to sys.path at the top of scripts to ensure 'src' imports work when running scripts directly, which is a minimal local-dev convenience change for samples. - Keeps scripts runnable directly without requiring users to install the package; acceptable for a sample repo.
- **17-rare-disease-literature-search** D004: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small for embeddings and will require OPENAI_API_KEY in env. - OpenAI embeddings are commonly used, integrate with LlamaIndex easily, and are convenient for a small sample; selecting text-embedding-3-small balances cost and quality for abstracts.
- **17-rare-disease-literature-search** D005: I chose OpenAI gpt-3.5-turbo for the answer-generation LLM. - gpt-3.5-turbo is broadly available and adequate for a sample; it reduces cost for CI and demo runs while still producing good quality answers.
- **17-rare-disease-literature-search** D008: I chose to require 'llama-index>=0.9.0' in requirements.txt rather than pinning to a single patch version. - Using a minimum compatible version preserves flexibility while keeping the package recent enough to support TokenTextSplitter and OpenAI embeddings; exact pins can be adjusted later by maintainers.
- **17-rare-disease-literature-search** D009: I chose FastAPI for the HTTP API (POST /query). - FastAPI provides async support, automatic docs, and is popular for Python APIs; it integrates well with uvicorn for local runs.
- **17-rare-disease-literature-search** D010: I chose to insert a few dummy documents with zero vectors for verification instead of running the full OpenAI-backed seed, to avoid requiring an OPENAI_API_KEY during automated verification. - This lets us verify collection creation, index creation, and $search behavior (score metadata) without relying on external API keys during the build run. We will document how to run the full seed with OpenAI in the README.
- **17-seasonal-trend-spotter-agent** D003: I chose pymongo (official MongoDB Python driver) pinned to a recent 4.x release (pymongo>=4.4,<5). - pymongo is the recommended synchronous driver and easier for a small sample; it aligns with the documentdb-driver skill guidance to use a singleton MongoClient.
- **17-seasonal-trend-spotter-agent** D005: I chose Bing Web Search (Azure Cognitive Services) as the web-search provider and to read credentials from BING_API_KEY and BING_ENDPOINT environment variables. If those aren't set the tool will return a small mocked set of trend signals. - Bing Web Search is well-documented, commonly available to Azure users, and maps cleanly to an environment-variable credential pattern. Providing a mock fallback ensures the sample runs without external keys.
- **17-seasonal-trend-spotter-agent** D006: I chose a compound index on { category: 1, sale_date: 1 } to support the aggregation grouping and range queries. - Compound index aligns with ESR pattern (equality on category, range on sale_date) and will speed seasonal aggregation by category.
- **17-seasonal-trend-spotter-agent** D007: I chose OpenAI (public OpenAI API) for embeddings and the chat LLM, using environment variable OPENAI_API_KEY. I will use the embedding model 'text-embedding-3-small' and the chat model 'gpt-3.5-turbo'. - OpenAI is commonly available, integrates with LangChain, and 'text-embedding-3-small' is a sensible embedding choice for sample-sized vector work. Using 'gpt-3.5-turbo' avoids requiring enterprise access to GPT-4 variants.
- **17-seasonal-trend-spotter-agent** D008: I chose not to create/use a vector index in this sample (vector search is out of scope); the sample will combine aggregation (sales velocity) and web signals using simple numeric scoring and optional embeddings for ranking if OPENAI_API_KEY is provided in the environment. If embeddings are used, they are computed client-side and not stored in the DB. - Keep sample simple and focused on combining DocumentDB aggregation with web signals; avoid creating persistent vector indexes on the production cluster in a sample.
- **17-stress-testing-multi-agent-runner** D009: I chose Python 3.11 for the sample runtime. - 3.11 is a widely supported modern LTS for samples and compatible with LangChain and PyMongo; it balances novelty with library compatibility.
- **18-360-data-platform-cdp-orchestration** D004: I chose a two-collection model: 'staging_customers' for raw ingest and 'customers' for normalized canonical profiles, plus 'workflows' for run metadata. - This keeps seed idempotent and separates raw inputs from canonical profiles, making dedup/merge simpler to demonstrate.
- **18-360-data-platform-cdp-orchestration** D007: I chose to implement a runtime fallback: if OPENAI_API_KEY (or equivalent LangChain provider config) is not present, the dedup agent will use a deterministic heuristic (email equality + normalized name equality) and skip LLM calls. This keeps the sample runnable without LLM keys while still demonstrating LLM integration when keys are provided. - To ensure the sample runs in environments without LLM credentials and to respect secret-handling rules.
- **18-360-data-platform-cdp-orchestration** D008: I chose to create the basic indexes (hashed on email and text on normalized_name) via a small pymongo script (ensure_indexes.py) rather than attempting complex vector index creation via the MCP tool in this run. I'll still attempt a vector index creation via the documentdb_create_index MCP tool afterwards and record any failures. - Pymongo create_index is straightforward and will succeed for hashed and text indexes; MCP can be used as a second step for vector index where supported and verified.
- **18-360-data-platform-cdp-orchestration** D020: I chose OpenAI (OPENAI_API_KEY) as the LLM provider and LangChain's ChatOpenAI for LLM-assisted matching, with environment-variable configuration. - OpenAI is the common default and works with LangChain; reading API key from env avoids storing secrets.
- **18-360-data-platform-cdp-orchestration** D021: I chose text-embedding-3-small (1536 dims) as the embedding model and set embedding dimensionality to 1536. - This is a reasonable, modern OpenAI embedding model and matches vector-index expectations; it keeps vector sizes moderate for a demo.
- **18-360-data-platform-cdp-orchestration** D024: I chose a single 'customers' collection with embedded arrays/objects for addresses and firmographics. - Embedding simplifies read queries for customer 360, follows documentdb-data-modeling guidance for read-heavy profiles and keeps the sample simple.
- **18-360-data-platform-cdp-orchestration** D031: I chose a single `customers` collection in database `sample_customer_360_18` with embedded addresses, a `firmographics` subdocument, `schemaVersion`, `normalized_name`, an `embedding` vector field, and `workflow_history` audit array. - Azure DocumentDB favors denormalization/read-optimized designs; this schema keeps customer profile together and supports vector search and enrichment.
- **18-adverse-event-reporting-pipeline** D003: I chose FastAPI for the app surface with POST /reports and GET /reports/top-events. - FastAPI is lightweight, async-friendly (Beanie uses async), and familiar to Python developers; it maps well to the POST/GET endpoints requested.
- **18-adverse-event-reporting-pipeline** D005: I chose OpenAI (openai-python) as the LLM provider and to read API key from OPENAI_API_KEY. - OpenAI is widely available and has a simple JSON-output prompt pattern; the app will degrade to a direct-insert fallback when the API key is absent.
- **18-adverse-event-reporting-pipeline** D007: I chose to store raw_text and created_at in addition to extracted structured fields (drug, event, severity, onset_date). - Preserving raw_text and timestamp aids debugging and re-extraction without losing source content.
- **18-chargeback-dispute-multi-agent** D006: I chose separate collections: 'transactions', 'communications', 'claims', and 'decisions' within database 'sample_fintech_18'. Documents will include schemaVersion and concise fields (ids, timestamps, amount, message, metadata). - Keeps data normalized for this demo, fits expected access patterns (evidence fetch uses transactionId), and supports schemaVersioning guidance.
- **18-chargeback-dispute-multi-agent** D007: I chose to implement deterministic, rule-based policy reasoning by default (so the sample can run without LLM keys) and provide optional hooks to call OpenAI (embeddings + LLM) when API keys are present. For docs, I will recommend OpenAI text-embedding-3-small (1536 dims) and a reasoning LLM such as gpt-4o-mini as examples. - Ensures the demo is verifiable in environments without API keys while showing how to enable LLM/embeddings if the user wants to experiment.
- **18-chargeback-dispute-multi-agent** D009: I chose to include lightweight Jest unit tests that mock agents so CI can run without a live cluster; seed and sample-run instructions will still show how to run against the live cluster. - Enables automated verification in environments that lack cluster credentials while keeping the seed script available for manual verification.
- **18-chargeback-dispute-multi-agent** D012: I chose the official 'mongodb' Node.js driver at a current 5.x release. - MongoDB Node driver 5.x supports MongoDB 7.0 wire protocol per the readiness report; matches DocumentDB compatibility.
- **18-chargeback-dispute-multi-agent** D014: I chose collections: 'transactions', 'communications', 'claims', and 'decisions' in database 'sample_fintech_18'. - Keeps responsibilities separate: transactions & communications are evidence, claims record incoming disputes, decisions store final outcomes; matches sample workflow.
- **18-chargeback-dispute-multi-agent** D016: I chose deterministic rule-based policy agent by default with optional OpenAI LLM usage when OPENAI_API_KEY is provided. - Allows the sample to demonstrate the multi-agent flow without requiring API keys while showing where to plug in an LLM for richer policy reasoning.
- **18-chargeback-dispute-multi-agent** D021: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small (1536 dims) if embeddings are used; but the sample will not create a vector index by default to keep the sample minimal. - Embedding model choice determines vector index dimensions; to avoid mis-sized indexes and since semantic search is not strictly required, I will default to a lexical multi-field approach and keep embedding/model choice documented for extension.
- **18-returns-refund-workflow-orchestration** D004: I chose OpenAI-compatible provider via LangChain Chat model with mock fallback when OPENAI_API_KEY is not set. - LangChain supports OpenAI-compatible models and this choice eases mapping to common environments; adding a mock fallback avoids unexpected costs in demo runs and allows verification without calling external LLMs.
- **18-returns-refund-workflow-orchestration** D005: I chose to use a mock LLM output for demo/test runs when OPENAI_API_KEY is not present; allow live calls if OPENAI_API_KEY is provided. - Avoids unexpected costs and flakiness in CI; still allows real LLM runs if the developer opts in by setting OPENAI_API_KEY.
- **18-returns-refund-workflow-orchestration** D008: I chose to create a simple unique index on orders.orderId and indexes on returns.orderId and returns.createdAt. - Sample doesn't require vector or full-text features; simple indexes support efficient lookups for the orchestration scenario and demonstrate index creation.
- **18-sanctions-list-screening-agent** D004: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small as the embedding provider and set embedding dimension to 1536. - OpenAI text-embedding-3-small is commonly available, cost-effective for samples, and maps to dimension 1536 used in many examples; this keeps the sample simple and reproducible. The .env.example will document OPENAI_API_KEY and EMBEDDING_DIM=1536.
- **18-sanctions-list-screening-agent** D008: I chose vector weight 0.7 and regex weight 0.3 for combining signals, with thresholds: high >=0.8, medium >=0.5, else low. - Vector similarity is the primary signal for semantic match; regex provides high-precision exact/fuzzy textual match. The 70/30 split gives preference to vector similarity but lets exact regex boost the score. Chosen thresholds are simple and suitable for a sample app; production systems should tune them on labeled data.
- **19-care-coordination-multi-agent** D011: I chose not to use vector retrieval or create vector indexes for this sample; the sample will use standard text aggregation and an LLM for synthesis. - To reduce complexity, avoid having to pick an embedding model/dimension and create vector indexes; the orchestrator can synthesize from fetched text via an LLM. This keeps the sample simpler and avoids needing OPENAI_API_KEY to verify DB operations.
- **19-care-coordination-multi-agent** D016: I chose PyMongo (pymongo) >=4.7 as the MongoDB driver. - PyMongo is the official MongoDB driver and recent 4.x releases support MongoDB 7.0 wire protocol features; the documentdb-driver skill recommends a singleton MongoClient.
- **19-care-coordination-multi-agent** D019: I chose to include simple single-field indexes for patient lookups and NOT create a vector index in the sample runtime. The code supports adding vector retrieval later and documents how to add vector indexes. - No LLM/embedding credentials were provided in the environment, so creating a vector index tied to a chosen embedding dimension may be misleading; a single-field index suffices for this tutorial and low-volume sample data. The README documents how to add vector indexes later.
- **19-care-coordination-multi-agent** D028: I chose to pin reasonable, recent package versions for requirements.txt: pymongo==4.8.0, langchain==0.0.349, openai==1.36.0, fastapi==0.95.2, uvicorn==0.22.0, python-dotenv==1.0.0, pytest==7.4.0, pytest-mock==3.11.0. - Pinning specific versions reduces surprise for users; chosen versions are recent and compatible with modern Python and driver, and are commonly used with LangChain and OpenAI SDKs. They can be adjusted by users as needed.
- **19-cross-sell-recommendation-beanie** D003: I chose to pin package versions in requirements.txt: beanie>=1.19,<2.0, motor==3.1.1, pymongo==4.7.0, fastapi==0.100.0, uvicorn==0.22.0, openai==1.0.0, numpy==1.26.0. - Pinning increases reproducibility while using version ranges for Beanie to allow small updates; selected versions are recent stable releases likely compatible.
- **19-cross-sell-recommendation-beanie** D006: I chose to store both product embeddings (collection 'products') and product-pair embeddings (collection 'product_pairs'). - Storing product embeddings simplifies computing a basket embedding (average of product embeddings) and improves cross-sell scoring while still storing pair embeddings for direct similarity; it's straightforward in the seed script and keeps retrieval efficient.
- **19-cross-sell-recommendation-beanie** D007: I chose OpenAI 'text-embedding-3-small' with 1536 dimensions for embeddings. - This model is efficient, widely available, and 1536 dims are a good trade-off for a sample; document will note how to change model via env var.
- **19-cross-sell-recommendation-beanie** D012: I chose to make startup skip DB initialization when DOCUMENTDB_URI is not set (so unit tests that exercise request validation can run without a live DB). - Skipping init when the env var is absent allows lightweight validation tests to run locally while still requiring DOCUMENTDB_URI for full integration. This is a pragmatic change for developer ergonomics in the sample code, and the README documents that a live cluster is needed for full functionality.
- **19-insider-threat-detection-pipeline** D004: I chose OpenAI embedding model 'text-embedding-3-small' with 1536 dimensions for the sample. - Smaller model reduces compute/cost for sample while providing reasonable quality; 1536 dims is common for 'small' models; we'll record dimensionality and use it to create the vector index.
- **19-insider-threat-detection-pipeline** D005: I chose a cosine similarity threshold of 0.7 for flagging significant deviations (cosine similarity between recent embedding and historical centroid). - A 0.7 cosine threshold is a reasonable illustrative cutoff for semantic drift in embeddings for a sample; this is documented and configurable in config.
- **19-option-chain-data-store** D003: I chose FastAPI for the HTTP API framework. - FastAPI is modern, easy to run with uvicorn, has automatic validation with Pydantic, and is suitable for a small sample app.
- **19-option-chain-data-store** D004: I chose pymongo 4.x and specifically pinned pymongo==4.5.0 in requirements.txt. - Pymongo 4.x is current stable major version; 4.5.0 is a safe recent choice compatible with synchronous and async patterns, and the connection skill suggests PyMongo 4.x.
- **19-option-chain-data-store** D008: I chose a single collection named 'options' with expiry stored as a BSON Date (datetime.date/datetime), strike as numeric, and underlying as string. - Storing expiry as a date allows efficient range queries and index use; one collection keeps the model simple for sample app.
- **19-proactive-outreach-orchestration** D003: I chose the official mongodb npm driver (latest stable compatible with Node.js 24, e.g., mongodb 5.x/6.x) and will instantiate a singleton MongoClient. - Official driver is recommended by DocumentDB skills and Node.js ecosystem for MongoDB-compatible clusters.
- **19-proactive-outreach-orchestration** D005: I chose to provide an idempotent Node.js seed script that uses the mongodb driver (reads DOCUMENTDB_URI) and also provide a script using documentdb MCP server as a backup for verification. - Node.js seed script is part of the sample app and runnable by developers; MCP backup allows verification via the provided MCP server tools if the driver cannot connect from the runner.
- **19-proactive-outreach-orchestration** D007: I chose text-embedding-3-small with 1536 dimensions for embeddings in the sample and for configuring the vector index dimensions. - Need a concrete embedding dimension for the vector-index schema; text-embedding-3-small is commonly used and 1536 dims is a reasonable sample dimension. This is not DocumentDB-specific and is therefore a model_default choice.
- **19-proactive-outreach-orchestration** D011: I chose the official mongodb npm package (latest stable compatible with Node.js 24). - Official driver is recommended; will instantiate a singleton MongoClient.
- **19-proactive-outreach-orchestration** D014: I chose a denormalized document model with three collections: customers, events, and outreach_logs. - Denormalization is appropriate for read-heavy orchestration flows; keeps sample simple to query and seed.
- **19-proactive-outreach-orchestration** D017: I chose to implement a CLI listener (long-running) and a minimal Express REST API exposing GET /logs for recent outreach logs. - Provides both a way to run the orchestrator and to inspect results programmatically.
- **20-identity-resolution-multi-agent** D003: I chose Motor (AsyncIOMotorClient) together with Beanie ODM. - Beanie requires Motor for its async ORM support, so to use Beanie models and follow library patterns we must use Motor as the async client.
- **20-identity-resolution-multi-agent** D004: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small with 1536 dimensions for embeddings. - Pick a concrete, commonly used embedding model so vector-index dimensions and code are deterministic; 1536 is standard for several OpenAI embedding models.
- **20-identity-resolution-multi-agent** D005: I chose OpenAI gpt-3.5-turbo for LLM-assisted merge/decision steps. - Use a broadly available and low-cost chat model for merge decision logic in the sample; keep it configurable via env var OPENAI_API_MODEL.
- **20-identity-resolution-multi-agent** D009: I chose OpenAI text-embedding-3-small with 1536 dimensions for embeddings, stored in document field `embedding` and used to create an HNSW vector index. - text-embedding-3-small is commonly used in samples for semantic similarity and has manageable dimensionality (1536) for example datasets; HNSW offers a good recall/latency balance.
- **20-identity-resolution-multi-agent** D010: I chose OpenAI API for embeddings and LLM calls; API key provided through OPENAI_API_KEY environment variable. - OpenAI is widely used, matches tooling (embeddings + chat), and simplifies sample setup; using OPENAI_API_KEY keeps credentials out of code.
